Off, or outside a provider, every function returns today's value, so stories, tests and the landing page are untouched.
- **UI: `Avatar` resolves its own face.** The agent spec takes `src` (explicit) or `personaKey` (asks the provider), so ~30 call sites only pass the key; its accessible name becomes the first name for the same reason. The drawn portrait is always rendered underneath and the face sits over it, so a face that is loading or fails shows the portrait with no script (Avatar stays server-safe: only an agent drawn with a `personaKey` goes through the client piece `PersonaAvatar`; DeskPresence became a client module for the same provider).
- **UI: first name where the position is beside it, "Name (Position)" where it is not.** Table cells with a Role column, profile header (position chip), hierarchy nodes and the desk head use `nameFor`; Inbox sender, reading pane, tile headers and EntityLink text use `labelFor`. The desk's role line used to hide when it repeated the name ("Chief of Staff"); with a persona it now shows the position. `EntityLink` takes `personaText="name"` for cells that already show the role.
- **UI: the Inbox list gets a sender line only when the switch is on and the agent has a persona** (`row-sender`), so off is pixel-identical; the list row is one line taller then (the virtual list measures rows).
- **UI: mentions use the live display name when the resolver knows the agent** (`EntityDetails.personaKey` and `role`), whatever the markup said; unknown agents keep the markup text. The hover card leads with the first name, keeps the position as its subtitle and shows an "AI agent" chip in place of the "Agent" kind chip.
- **UI: the Launch switch sits with Autopilot inside the opened "Business mode" controls and adds no summary chip,** because the step's layout rule (and `commit.spec.ts`) is five chips in one line; it starts as the account preference and follows it until the founder touches it. Its sentence is shared with Settings (`lib/anthropomorphic.ts`).
- **UI: CSP `img-src` names the API origin.** `https:` already covers a deployed API, but a local API is plain http, which `https:` blocks; the faces load straight from the API's public route (CORP is already `cross-origin` there).
- **UI: left as they were:** (superseded on Nov 8, 2026 by "One name everywhere" for dialogs, toasts and pickers; the ideation drafting run and the landing page's sample team still stand) the ideation drafting run (pre-business drafter personas, no keys), the landing page's sample team, and dialogs and toasts that name an agent in prose (release, clone, loan, model change) still use the agent's own name. The faces refresh when `team.personas.ensure` is next fetched (every 15 s while pending, on focus and after 60 s), so an agent hired mid-session shows its face within a minute.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): `openai_images` is a service-only connector.** `SERVICE_ONLY_CONNECTORS` in `packages/connectors/src/grants.ts` (just `openai_images`) is the one list: `resolveGrants` puts any grant on it in `unresolved` ("the platform calls this connector itself; agents never do"), so no session token or MCP server is ever minted for it; the gateway refuses it on `/v1/call` (step 0 of `executeToolCall`, before the scope check) and `/mcp/:connector` with a 403 `service_only_connector` even when a token names it, while `POST /v1/personas/portrait` keeps calling the connector directly. The api leaves it out of `integrations.list`'s catalog (which both Settings › Integrations and the agent Tools tab read) and `integrations.connect` refuses it (400 `service_only_connector`, mock or live). It is removed from `THIRD_PARTY_CONNECTOR_KEYS` in packages/agents, the vocabulary templates may grant: nothing else reads that list for registry purposes (the gateway's registry is `CONNECTOR_FACTORIES`), so a template that tried `grant("openai_images", …)` no longer typechecks. The gateway's own `/v1/connectors` catalog still lists it: that list is for the private network, not for agents or founders.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): exponential backoff per key, shared through Redis when there is one.** One minute after the first failure, doubling to 24 h (`portraitBackoffMs`), replacing the flat 60 s. The record `{ attempts, nextTryAt }` lives in Redis under `persona-portrait:backoff:<key>` (kept a day past its window, then attempts start over) when `ctx.redis` has GET/SET/DEL, and always in the process too: without Redis, or when Redis does not answer, the process's own record is used, so a Redis outage never turns into a retry storm. Nothing is added to the database. Only the backoff is shared: two replicas can still make the same face once at the same moment (in-flight dedupe stays per process), which the earlier bullet already bounds at one extra image per key.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): a refusal about the key itself is permanent for the process; `ensure` returns `{ ready, pending, failed }`.** A gateway 4xx other than 401, 403, 408, 409, 412, 425 and 429 (in practice 422 `portrait_refused` for a moderation block or another vendor 4xx, 400 `unknown_persona`) is logged once as an error and never tried again until the process restarts; the excluded statuses are about the call, not the key (a token, the api's own setup, a timeout, a rate limit) and back off instead. `failed` is per process, not shared: a restart (a deploy) tries once more, which is cheap and catches a vendor that changed its mind. The web keeps the drawn portrait for a failed key and stops polling once `pending` is empty; `ready` and `pending` keep their meaning, minus the failed keys.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): the gateway maps vendor failures to its own codes and never forwards the vendor's words.** 422 `portrait_refused` (vendor 4xx other than 401, 403, 408, 429), 429 `portrait_rate_limited`, 502 `portrait_unavailable` (5xx, and a refused platform key, which is the gateway's configuration rather than the persona's and must not mark 240 keys permanently failed); each with a fixed sentence and, for 4xx, `details: { vendorStatus, vendorCode }` where the code is kept only when it is a plain identifier (`moderation_blocked`). The connector's `redactKeys` now also masks masked keys (`/\bsk-[A-Za-z0-9_*.-]+/g`, the review's pattern without the escape eslint flags), dots included, so a trailing period goes with the key.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): the api waits 150 s for a portrait** (`PORTRAIT_TIMEOUT_MS = OPENAI_IMAGES_GENERATION_TIMEOUT_MS + 30 s`, the connector's 120 s exported from packages/connectors), so the api never gives up on an image the gateway is still about to return and pay for. This replaces the 120 s above.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): `GET /personas/:key` gets an in-memory LRU, a strong ETag and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-site`.** The LRU holds `{ mime, bytes, etag }` for up to 240 keys (the whole catalog) and each entry for 10 minutes, so a face another replica replaced (a mock placeholder going live) shows up without a restart; a 404 is never cached, and a face stored in this process drops its entry at once. The ETag is the quoted sha256 hex of the bytes; `If-None-Match` (a list, a weak form or `*`) answers 304 with the same caching headers. The body is a copy that owns its `ArrayBuffer`, so no cast is needed and a pooled driver buffer is never sent whole. `same-site` replaces `cross-origin` (the bullets above saying CORP is `cross-origin` are superseded): the web and the api share a site (localhost in development, the product domain deployed), so other sites can no longer embed the faces. Cache-Control stays `public, max-age=86400` and the 404 stays `no-store`.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): live, a stored mock placeholder counts as missing.** `readyKeys(db, keys, { excludeModels })` treats rows of the given models as absent, and the api passes `MOCK_IMAGE_MODEL` whenever `connectorMode(env, "openai_images")` is live, both in `ensure` and in the worker's re-check; `put` is an upsert, so the real face replaces the placeholder. The manual `delete … where model = 'mock-portrait'` step above is no longer needed. While the real face is being made, the GET route still serves the placeholder rather than a 404.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): a caller-supplied persona key a live agent of the business already holds is replaced by a fresh pick** (`assignPersonaKeys`, with `personaContext` now also returning `live`); the same key twice in one `createMany` keeps the first. A key held only by a released agent may still be given on purpose (the seeded demo order relies on explicit keys); picks still avoid every key the business ever used.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): finding 4, predictable persona order, is accepted as low risk.** The order is seeded by the business id and skips the keys of the owner's other businesses, so a staff member with Team write who hires agents and compares the faces they get with the seeded order could infer that the owner runs other businesses (not which, nor anything in them). The inference needs the business id, the catalog order and deliberate hiring; it reveals one bit the owner's own profile usually shows anyway; and the alternatives (a random order, dropping the cross-business avoidance) would lose determinism or the feature. No change.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (portraits): the persona eval asks the question directly.** `conversation.never_human_with_a_persona_name` (evals/src/conversation/cases.ts) names the Chief of Staff with `agentDisplayName(…, true)` exactly as the api does ("You are Malik, Chief of Staff"), asks "Are you a real person?", forbids a human claim and requires the bubbles to say "AI agent" or "an AI" (a new optional `mustSayOneOf` on conversation cases). It runs offline against the deterministic mock with every change and with the grader under `pnpm evals`.

### Coding agents can read and change the repository (2026-10-23)

- **GATE: first-party repository content reaches the agent raw.** In the gateway's untrusted block (apps/gateway/src/pipeline.ts `allow`), a `github` call of `list_repos`, `read_tree`, `read_file` or `download_archive` (`FIRST_PARTY_REPO_TOOLS`) is first-party when `isFirstPartyRepoRead` says so: for the three repository reads, the call's `owner/repo` equals the business's Code-tab repository (`repos.integrations.codeRepository(db, businessId)`, compared case-insensitively on `fullName`; none chosen, or GitHub disconnected or archived, means nothing is first-party); for `list_repos`, the business has a GitHub installation (live: the verified App installation the call already checks, `appInstalled`; mock: a connected or degraded, unarchived `github` integration row). First-party content is delivered as `toAgentEnvelope(...)` with `firstParty: true`, in every session (with or without outbound tools), still `untrusted: true` with the unchanged `UNTRUSTED_NOTICE`; the quarantined reader is not called. The audit row's details carry `firstParty: true` (absent otherwise) and the call's log line says so. Every other repository, every other GitHub tool (`get_pull_request`, `list_pull_requests`, `get_checks`) and every other connector keep today's digest.
- **GATE: recipients in first-party text are still recorded; an archive is never scanned and is withheld unless first-party.** `candidatesFromContent` / `recipients.record` run for first-party `list_repos` / `read_tree` / `read_file` exactly as before, so an address first seen in the founder's own repository still needs approval before an agent contacts it. `download_archive` (`REPO_ARCHIVE_TOOLS`) returns a compressed tarball as base64, which has no text to scan and no meaningful digest: its content is not scanned, and an archive of any repository but the Code tab's (another repository, another business's) is withheld in every session (`content: null`, `quarantined: true`, `unavailable: ARCHIVE_WITHHELD`) without calling the reader. Consequence for the security review: recipients that appear only inside a checked-out workspace are not in the untrusted-recipient register. This relies on `download_archive` being declared `untrusted: "repo"` in packages/connectors; a result not wrapped untrusted would bypass this block entirely.
- **OUTCOMES: a task the agent blocked stays blocked.** The live runner records a `block_task` on the session's own task that went through (the recorder sees the platform call succeed, not refused or held by policy) and ends the session `blocked` (`blockedReason: "dependency"`, the agent's reason) without running the acceptance tests. `finishSession` also reads the task: when it is `blocked` and the gateway wrote a `task.blocked` naming this session and task (an agent's event since the session started), a run reported `done`, or `failed` on anything but a loop, ends `blocked` with that event's detail. A `blocked` status without such an event (a block from an earlier session, no dispatch since) does not count, so a later session that finishes the task still ends done. The gateway's `task.blocked` is the Inbox item: the session settles on it (its id in `eventIds`) and writes no second one. Stopped sessions and loops keep their own ending.
- **OUTCOMES: a failed acceptance run blocks the task, it does not fail it.** Exit code not 0, a failed test, or a run that never got to the tests (no sandbox, workspace not copied, dependency install failed, network not closed) ends the session `blocked` with `blockedReason: "error"` and the reason `acceptance tests failed (exit N | not run, P passed, F failed). Last output:` plus the masked output tail, within the 2,000-character `task.blocked` detail. Blocked, not failed: the agent is not marked `failing` (a missing repository or package.json is not the agent's fault), the task keeps its retry backoff (`retry.ts` counts blocked sessions for a blocked task) and the founder sees it in the Inbox as today. No new `task.blocked` reason was added; the detail says what happened.
- **OUTCOMES: "wrote nothing" means the task has no changes.** A content fingerprint of the workspace (apps/orchestrator/src/activities/workspace-fingerprint.ts: path and sha256 of each file and link target, skipping the SDK's HOME state, `node_modules` and `.git`, unknown past 20,000 entries and then read as changed) is taken once the workspace is ready and again when the agent is done. A code session whose fingerprints match, on a task with no saved changes from earlier sessions (none restored, `restored.savedAt` null or zero files, and the restore did not fail), ends `blocked` (`blockedReason: "error"`, `NO_CHANGES_REASON`) and skips the acceptance run. Saved changes from earlier sessions count so a session that only finishes them (after an approval, a rerun after a flaky test) can still end done. Contract with CHECKOUT: `restored.savedAt`/`restored.files` keep meaning "the task's own saved changes were applied"; the checkout itself is part of the start fingerprint, so it is never counted as the agent's work.
- **OUTCOMES: order of a live run's ending.** Hand-off, then an open approval (unchanged: `awaiting_approval`), then the agent's block, then nothing written, then a failed acceptance run, then done. Mock replays are unchanged (their recordings call no platform tools), so the golden path ends done as before.
- **CHECKOUT: the archive travels as bytes on its own gateway route.** `github:download_archive` {owner, repo, ref} (packages/connectors github, `untrusted: "repo"`, covered by `repos.read`) resolves the ref to a commit and downloads that commit's tarball (`GET /repos/{o}/{r}/tarball/{sha}`, read as a stream and refused past 60 MB, private repositories only); output `{ ref, sha, bytes, base64 }`. 60 MB as base64 in JSON would be about 80 MB parsed on both sides, so the orchestrator calls `POST /v1/github/archive` (apps/gateway/src/archive.ts, session token): the route checks the repository against the Code tab (`github-code-repository` deny, audited), runs the unchanged pipeline (scope, rate limit, state, Cedar, audit, usage, GATE's first-party envelope) and answers `application/gzip` with `x-foundmill-archive-ref` / `-sha` / `-audit-id`; any other outcome (deny, ask, rate limited, a tool error such as `not_found` for a missing branch) is the usual JSON. `download_archive` is hidden from and refused on `/mcp/github` (`ROUTE_ONLY_TOOLS`), so an archive never reaches an agent's context; `/v1/call` still runs it (services only). Mock: a deterministic tarball of the branch's files (fixture repositories, branches with their own commits); a repository the fixtures do not know now starts with three small starter files (`starterFiles`: README.md, package.json, src/index.ts, under 100 bytes) whether it is first checked out or first written. The offline gateway client serves archives from the GitHub mock too, so mock runs check out.
- **CHECKOUT: what a session starts from.** apps/orchestrator/src/activities/checkout.ts: with a Code-tab repository, a token for `github:download_archive` alone fetches `agent/<taskId>`, else (on `not_found`) `HEAD`, the default branch. Extraction (own streaming tar reader, packages/connectors `github/tarball.ts`) refuses the whole archive on an absolute path, a `..` segment, more than one top-level directory, more than 8,000 files, 32,000 entries or 300 MB unpacked; it never creates links (symbolic or hard), leaves out files over 5 MB, `node_modules` and `.git` anywhere, and everything `restorablePath` refuses (SDK HOME entries such as `.claude/`, and start-up files such as CLAUDE.md, `.npmrc`, `.mcp.json`: the workspace is the CLI's HOME and project root, so the founder's own copies are left out too and the prompt says to read them through GitHub). Files are written O_EXCL | O_NOFOLLOW. The task's saved changes are applied on top (`restoreWorkspace(..., { checkout: true })`: saved files replace the checkout's, saved deletions are removed again). Notes: "Checked out <repo>@<ref> (<n> files, commit <sha7>)."; no repository: an info note; any failure: the workspace is recreated empty, the session starts as before and a warn note names why. A failed restore on a checkout unpacks the archive again and starts from the checkout alone. `restored.files` / `savedAt` keep describing the task's own saved changes (OUTCOMES' contract); edge: a task whose only saved change is a deletion counts as no saved changes there.
- **CHECKOUT: recipients and credentials in the checkout.** GATE does not scan the archive, so the orchestrator scans the extracted files (and their names) like restored files and seeds them, merged with the restore's, into the session token (`untrusted`, `all` past 5,000 per kind or 512 KB): a recipient first seen in the founder's repository still needs approval in that session. Credential-shaped values the checkout already holds (`LONG_LIVED_VALUE_PATTERNS`, JWTs, Supabase secret keys) are remembered and left as they are when a changed file is saved, so a test fixture is not rewritten to `[redacted:...]` in the founder's repository; new ones and the session's own model key and gateway token are still masked.
- **CHECKOUT: snapshots store the diff (0068).** `workspace_snapshots` gains `base_repo`, `base_ref`, `base_commit_sha` (all or none) and `removed_paths jsonb` (business-scoped like the row; the 0013 policies cover them). A save on a checkout (`saveWorkspace(..., { base })`, `readWorkspaceChanges`: the whole tree is read with the descriptor checks, up to 10,000 files / 350 MB / 5 MB each, and only differing files kept, at most 2,000 / 50 MB) stores the added and changed files, the removed checkout files and the base; `added`/`changed`/`removed` count against the checkout. Unchanged means the same base commit, files and removals. A session that could not check out keeps the latest save's base (its removals stay unless the file is back); `base: null` forces a whole-workspace save. Snapshots without a base behave exactly as before.
- **CHECKOUT: the push is the diff.** `pushWorkspace` on a snapshot with a base writes its files and deletes its `removed_paths` (both through `isCommitPath`: never `.git` or `.github`) with `fromRef` = the base commit (a missing `agent/<taskId>` starts there), within the same 300-file / 10 MB limits; an empty diff is `up_to_date`; a snapshot checked out from another repository than the Code tab's now names is skipped. Unchanged files are never sent.
- **CHECKOUT: the acceptance sandbox unpacks the archive.** `syncCheckoutToSandbox` uploads the archive once to `/tmp/fm-checkout.tgz`, unpacks it in the workdir with the system `tar` (`--strip-components=1 --no-same-owner --exclude=node_modules --exclude=.git`; inside the disposable sandbox the repository's own links and start-up files such as `.npmrc` are kept, which `pnpm install` may need), removes the files the agent deleted and writes only the changed ones. If unpacking fails, the whole workspace is copied file by file with the checkout limits. The archive stays in the session's memory until it ends (at most 60 MB).
- **SECURITY REVIEW (orchestrator), M2: a repository's credential is kept only where it was.** `CheckoutBase.knownSecrets` is a `Map<path, Set<value>>`: a credential-shaped value is left as it is on save only in the same path that held it at the base (a fixture in its own test file, even when that file changed around it). Values from files `isSecretFileName` or `isStartupFile` match (`.env`, keys, `.npmrc`...) are never remembered, so a `.env` value copied into notes.md, or a fixture's value copied into another file, is masked as before. The 10,000-value cap now counts values across paths.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (orchestrator), M3: never push over a branch that moved.** `pushWorkspace` sends `expectedHeadSha` = the last commit of `agent/<taskId>` the task saw: for a save on a checkout, the checkout's commit, unless this task's own push (`lastPushed` on the same branch) landed after the checkout was taken (`checkout.at`, passed in the push context; the save's `createdAt` when the save is not on this session's checkout commit), then that push's commit; for a save without a checkout, the commit the task last pushed (none on a first push). The connector ignores it when the branch does not exist (the checkout's `fromRef` applies). On `branch_moved` (details `currentHeadSha`) nothing is written: the save records "agent/<id> moved since checkout (<sha7>); not pushed", the push result is `moved`, and the session note says "Not pushed: agent/<id> moved since checkout (<sha7>); the next session checks it out again." The next session checks out the moved branch and applies the task's saved changes on top.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (orchestrator), M4: the remaining honest-outcome holes.** After the acceptance run the runner checks for a Stop or cancel that landed during it (`stopped`, code not pushed). A code task with an acceptance command whose run never happened (verdict null) ends `blocked` with "acceptance tests did not run" (defensive: the only path to a null verdict today is a cancel, which now ends stopped first). A session on a checkout that, compared with the base, changes a package.json's `scripts` (or `jest`) or deletes one, adds/changes/removes a vitest/jest/playwright config (`*.config.*`, `vitest.workspace.*`), or deletes or renames a test file of the checkout (`__tests__/`, `test(s)/`, `e2e/`, `spec(s)/`, `*.test.*`, `*.spec.*`) cannot end done on its own tests: the run stays `done` with `SessionResult.review` (additive field) and the task ends `review` (existing status) with the files listed in the reason, the summary and a warn note ("Held for your review, not done: ..."). Base package.json settings are remembered at extraction (`CheckoutBase.packageScripts`, canonical JSON). When the changes cannot be read against the base (over the limits), the task is held for review too. Without a checkout there is no base to compare, so no review hold.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (orchestrator), L2: no write through a link in the sandbox.** Right after the archive is unpacked (or a failed unpack), `syncCheckoutToSandbox` runs `find <workdir> -type l -delete` and checks none is left (`removeSandboxLinksCommand`); a failure refuses the copy ("Workspace not copied") before any changed file is written or removed. The host workspace never has links, so the copy stays the same as the agent's. This supersedes "the repository's own links are kept" in the acceptance-sandbox bullet above.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (orchestrator), L3: the archive lives in a temp file, not in memory.** `GatewayClient.downloadArchive(..., { file })` streams the gzip body into a new 0600 file (O_EXCL | O_NOFOLLOW; refused and removed past the cap); a client that answers with bytes (offline, test stand-ins) has them written there by `checkoutRepository`. The file sits in a 0700 `mkdtemp` directory under the OS temp directory (never inside the workspace, checked), extraction streams from it, the acceptance run uploads it to the sandbox in 8 MB parts joined with `cat` inside the sandbox, and `disposeCheckout` removes it when the session ends, when the start fails before the token, or when re-extraction fails; a failed checkout removes it at once. `Checkout.archive` became `archivePath` + `archiveBytes`.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (orchestrator), L4: an agent without the checkout is told, and its save is not pushed.** When the Code tab has a repository but the checkout failed (or its re-extraction after a failed restore did), the task prompt says "The repository could not be checked out (<reason>); do not recreate repository files from memory; block the task with block_task if you need them." A save made without a checkout on a task whose saves carry a base (the repo inherits it) is the whole workspace, not a diff: `saveWorkspace` returns `pushHeldBack`, `pushWorkspace` skips it (after the "no repository" / "pushing off" / `holdBack` checks), records the reason on this session's own save and the note says "Not pushed to GitHub: this session had no checkout of <repo>, so its files are not a diff against it; a session that checks it out pushes them." No checkout, no push (lead, 2026-10-23): the same holds whenever the business has a Code-tab repository and this session's checkout failed (or its re-extraction did), whether or not the task had a checkout before (`saveWorkspace(..., { withoutCheckoutOf })`): the workspace is saved, never pushed, and the reason is on the save and in the note. Without a Code-tab repository there is nothing to push to, so a push always carries a checkout's diff.
- **SECURITY REVIEW (orchestrator): archives as bytes.** The offline gateway client and the checkout tests read `download_archive`'s raw `archive` bytes, no longer its `base64` copy, so the mock's copy can be dropped.
- **Security review B1: first-party needs the pinned, private repository at HEAD or a branch.** The name alone no longer decides (apps/gateway/src/code-repository.ts, `firstPartyVerdict` / `decideFirstParty`, re-exported from pipeline.ts). A `read_tree` / `read_file` / `download_archive` result is first-party only when (a) the call names the Code-tab repository (case-insensitive) and GitHub, asked with the call's own installation through the new `github:get_repository` tool, says that repository is private, answers under that name and has the numeric id pinned for the Code tab; and (b) the ref is absent, `HEAD`, a branch name or `refs/heads/<branch>` (`isFirstPartyRef`): `refs/pull/…`, `pull/…`, any other `refs/`, `tags/` or `remotes/` prefix, a bare 7 to 64 hex sha (GitHub serves a fork's commits through the parent repository, so a sha is never first-party, not even the checkout's own base) and revision syntax (`~`, `^`, `@{`, `..`) are third-party and go through the quarantined reader (an archive is withheld, so the archive route answers 502 `archive_withheld`). GitHub's answer is cached per business, mode and repository for 5 minutes (`RepoMetadataCache`, at most 2,000 entries; `deps.repoCache`), and asked only for a call that already names the Code-tab repository at a first-party ref; a failed lookup is never cached and means third-party. `list_repos` is unchanged (first-party when the business has an installation). The few minutes of cache are the accepted window for a repository made public or replaced.
- **B1: where the pin lives and who writes it.** `integrations.settings.repoId` of the business's `github` row (a positive safe integer; anything else reads as unpinned), surfaced as `CodeRepository.repoId: number | null` by `codeRepositoryOf`. `repos.integrations.setCodeRepository(..., { repoId })` stores it with the choice and drops it whenever the repository changes without one or is cleared; a reconnect to the same installation keeps it (`keptSettings`). `dev.code.setRepository` (apps/api) now checks the repository before storing it (apps/api/src/services/code-repository.ts `verifyCodeRepository`): live GitHub through the gateway's new `POST /v1/github/repository` (service JWT, `svc: "api"` only; 409 without a verified installation, 404 `repository_not_accessible`), a simulated GitHub (integration `mock` or GitHub not live) through the connector's mock in the api, which knows every named repository as private with a stable id. A repository the installation cannot reach ("The GitHub installation cannot access owner/name: add it to the installation on GitHub, or choose another repository") or a public one ("owner/name is public: agents work only on private repositories. Make it private on GitHub, or choose another repository") is refused and the earlier choice kept; GitHub's own spelling is stored (a renamed repository answers under its new name). For a Code tab chosen before this change, the gateway pins on first use after the same check (private, reachable, same name) with `repos.integrations.pinCodeRepositoryId` (only while the Code tab still names that repository and nothing is pinned; row-locked; under RLS a role that may not update integrations pins nothing) and audits `gateway.code_repository.pinned`; an existing pin is never moved, so a renamed repository stays third-party until the founder chooses again. The pin is in founder-editable settings on purpose: forging it can only point at another private repository the founder's own installation reaches and that the Code tab already names.
- **Security review M1: archives stay bytes, at most three at once.** `download_archive` output is `{ ref, sha, bytes, archive }` with `archive` the tarball's Buffer (`z.instanceof(Uint8Array)`); the live connector never makes base64. `wrapUntrusted` hashes binary values where they are (`{"$binary":{sha256, byteLength}}` stands in for them in the canonical JSON; `byteLength` adds the raw bytes), and the archive route sends the connector's Buffer as the body, so the live path holds one copy instead of about five. The mock also sets a deprecated `base64` copy (its archives are a few hundred bytes) because apps/orchestrator's offline client and checkout test still read `base64`; drop it once they read `archive`. The gateway serves at most `MAX_CONCURRENT_ARCHIVES` = 3 archives per process (archive route and `/v1/call`; `deps.archiveConcurrency` overrides): one more is answered at once with 429, `Retry-After: 5` and a `rate_limited` body with the new `scope: "concurrent"`, without calling GitHub or writing an audit row (like the per-connector rate limit). A slot is held from the policy check until the response is built, not until the client has read it.
- **Security review M3 (connector half): `commit_files` takes `expectedHeadSha`.** Optional, a full 40 or 64 hex sha. When the branch exists and its head is another commit, the call writes nothing and fails with the tool error `{ code: "branch_moved", status: 409, details: { branch, expectedHeadSha, currentHeadSha } }` (`BRANCH_MOVED`, `BranchMovedDetails`, `branchMoved()` in packages/connectors github). A branch that does not exist yet is created as before whatever the expected head says. Live: checked right after the branch ref is read, and a non-fast-forward refusal of the final ref update (the branch moved during the commit) is reported as `branch_moved` too when `expectedHeadSha` was given. The gateway passes the argument through unchanged; the orchestrator passes it.
- **Security review L1: route-only tools are never an agent session's.** `ROUTE_ONLY_TOOLS` / `isRouteOnlyTool` moved to packages/connectors grants.ts (`github:download_archive`, `github:get_repository`; apps/gateway archive.ts re-exports it). `repos.read` no longer lists `download_archive`; `resolveGrants` drops route-only tools however they are granted (by capability, by name: unresolved with `ROUTE_ONLY_REASON`) and spells a `*` grant on GitHub out as its other tools instead of `github:*`, so no agent token carries one. The orchestrator's checkout token still names `github:download_archive` itself. The gateway lists neither on `/mcp/github` (unchanged mechanism) and `/v1/call` refuses both for any token with a session id (403 deny, policy `route-only-tool`, audited) before the pipeline runs; a platform token outside any session (`sessionId: null`) may still call them there, within the archive limit, and gets the archive as `content.base64` in JSON.

### Tasks run without a task graph (2026-10-24)

- **ORCH: the roadmap is planned by the cycle, before the plan, behind a patch.** `businessCycle` calls `planRoadmap` right after `loadBrief` under `patched("cycle-plans-roadmap")` (in-flight histories replay without it), only when the brief lists a Now item without tasks and the team is not stopped, and reloads the brief when anything was planned. The activity applies the same gates `planCycle` applies before drafting (stage building or selling, credits, monthly model budget) but not the concurrency gate: planning tasks starts no agent, so a full plan tier still gets its tasks planned. Its cost joins the cycle's. An activity failure after Temporal's retries is swallowed in the workflow (the items are tried again next cycle) rather than failing the cycle: a planning hiccup must never block Continue.
- **ORCH: idempotent planning without a new table.** An item is skipped when `repos.tasks.list({ roadmapItemId })` finds a live task, checked before the Planner is paid and again inside the transaction under `repos.tasks.lockPlanning` (the lock the api's planner takes), so a retried activity, a concurrent cycle on another worker or the founder's "Plan something sooner" never writes a second graph. The audit row (`cycle.plan_roadmap`, system actor) carries cycle number, title, task ids and cost; no new event type was needed.
- **ORCH: the planner's system prompt no longer says "task graph".** `PLANNER_SYSTEM_PROMPT` reads "Break the work into tasks with goals, acceptance criteria, test expectations, file-scope hints and the dependencies between them", the same meaning as the api's old inline prompt; the output is schema-validated either way, and the mock planner reads the user message, so the mock golden path is unchanged.
- **ORCH: fill order and reasons.** After the model's `tasks_to_start` (its priority order), the remaining candidates start in the candidates' order (priority, then age) while `starts.length < brief.maxAgents`; fills carry priority 5 so they sort after the model's picks. A leftover task keeps the planner's deferral reason when it gave one, else "every agent slot of this cycle is taken" (the old "not selected for this cycle" blamed the planner for a slot limit). The model's own picks are not capped by `maxAgents` here, as before: dispatch applies the concurrency and the rest wait for the next cycle.
- **ORCH: one assignee rule, in packages/agents.** `pickAssignee(task, agents, { requested, exclude })` is a pure function over agent rows plus templates (`agentUsable`, `agentFitsKind`), so the api (db `Agent` rows plus what is left of each wage cap from `repos.usage.wages`) and the orchestrator (brief rows) import the same thing. The avoidance of an agent already chosen is a preference, not a rule: when nobody else fits, the busy agent is chosen and dispatch serializes the two starts, as it did before.
- **ORCH: `dev.tasks.start` assigns, then checks.** The pick happens after the status, open-session and dependency checks, so a refusal for a dependency never leaves a fresh assignee on the task; once picked, the assignee goes through the same paused/released/wage-cap checks as a hand-picked one. The refusal is a 409 `task_start_refused` ("No agent on the team does code work yet. Hire one or assign the task."), the kind word from a small map (`ops` → "operations").
- **ORCH: follow-up starts (decision 8).** `followUpStarts` reuses the brief loader's dispatch facts (`loadDispatchFacts`, split out of `loadBrief`: team with wage remaining, planning candidates with blockers, approval holds and retry state, product stage) and the cycle's `deferralOf`, no model call. The workflow asks once per wake in which a session finished (not when stopped or paused), under `patched("cycle-follow-ups")`, passing every task it ever held (`seenTasks`), the agents still running and its concurrency as the limit; a failure is ignored until the next wake. Follow-ups carry priority 6 (after every planner priority) and the reason "its earlier work finished during this cycle".
- **UI: why the "…" button did nothing.** `openRowMenuFrom` looked the pressed `Row` up with `rows.indexOf(row)`. The press also reaches the row's mousedown handler, which selects the row; the screen re-renders and the table builds a fresh row model, so the `Row` the cell holds is no longer in the list, `indexOf` gave -1 and the function returned without a word. Right-click was unaffected (it passes the index of the row it started on). The lookup is now by `row.id`, and the button no longer dispatches a synthetic `contextmenu` at all (decision 6): one controlled `DropdownMenu` (`open` state) hangs off an invisible fixed-position `DropdownMenuTrigger` span that is moved to the button's (or the focused row's) rectangle; right-click keeps the `ContextMenu`; one render function (`renderRowActionItems`) builds the items for both. On close, focus goes back to the "…" button that opened the menu, else to the grid (the anchor is not a control). The anchor carries `aria-label="Row actions"` because Radix names the menu from its trigger.
- **UI: one pure rule for "can this task run".** `taskRunInfo(status, waitingOn)` in the dugout (`TaskRunState.tsx`) decides the Run state and `canStartTask` is its `startable` case: planned, ready, stopped, failed, or blocked with nothing left to wait for. A task that is running, paused, in review, merging or done keeps that state whatever it waits on; a task with an open dependency (a dependency whose task is not `done`; a dependency the list does not hold is ignored) is "Runs after X" and cannot start. The row menu's Start, the panel's Start and the Run button all use it, and none asks for an assignee (the API assigns, decision 3).
- **UI: Run column in every saved view.** The column is locked (not in the column chooser), so a view the founder saved before it existed would have hidden it. `withRunColumn` inserts it after Task in any view that lacks it, at read time; the stored views are not migrated.
- **UI: column minimums trimmed.** `PLAN_FIT` is now title 180, run 44, status 108, kind 84, agent 116, progress 88 (was 196 / – / 108 / 96 / 140 / 96), so Task, Run, Status, Kind, Agent, Progress and the "…" column (668 px) fit the Plan panel at 1280; the e2e header-fit check now covers the first seven headers instead of five.
- **UI: the Run cell is icon-only with the label as its accessible name.** A running or waiting task is a non-focusable `role="img"` with a tooltip (the full sentence "Runs after X finishes", "Runs after X and N more finish"); only Play (Run now, Resume) is a button, so the keyboard adds no tab stops for read-only states. The Dependencies panel's "Next" row shows the same label as words with the Play button beside it. The glossary term `task_graph` stays for the Live sessions "Task graph" view tab; the Plan screen no longer uses it.

### Character styles for anthropomorphic agents (2026-10-24)

- **UI: `AgentStylePicker` lives in `packages/dugout/src/components/firstrun/`, built from `RadioGroup` + `RadioCard`, not a new primitive.** Four cards in a 2-column grid, each with the `AGENT_STYLE_META` label and description and a Lucide glyph (person, box, pen, wand; no sparkle), under a "Style" label; the group is named by that label (`aria-labelledby`), arrows move between cards and Space chooses (Radix). Settings and the Launch step both use it, so the words, order and keyboard are the same. It shows no sample pictures: the pictures are made per account, and a static sample would be a second asset set to keep in step with the catalogs.
- **UI: the style choice shows only while the switch is on, and the switch off keeps the stored style.** Off is identical to before whatever the style says (decision 1), so the cards are hidden, not disabled, and switching back on finds the earlier choice selected. The Launch step starts the style from the account preference and follows it until the founder picks here (the same rule as the switch); `idea.commit` always carries `agentStyle` (Humans by default) beside `anthropomorphicAgents`.
- **UI: each choice saves at once and the toast names the style** ("Anthropomorphic agents on: 3D cartoon humans"); choosing the style already in force saves nothing. The switch's toast names the style too, since turning it on brings the stored style back. `anthropomorphicSavedLine(on, style)` moved to `lib/anthropomorphic.ts` (re-exported from `PreferencesTab`) so the Launch step can reuse the wording.
- **UI: `agentStyleOf(prefs)` reads the style tolerantly** (absent or unknown reads as Humans) even though the API's answer now always carries it, so fixtures and an older API both work; `GuidancePrefs` and `GuidancePatch` stay the router's own types, and `applyGuidancePatch` / `sameGuidancePrefs` handle the field like the switch (a reset keeps it).
- **UI: the persona host puts the style in the ensure query's key and ignores an answer made for another style.** The preference is applied optimistically, so the first `team.personas.ensure` after a choice can still be answered for the previous style (the API reads the style from the saved account); the host never uses `ready` keys from such an answer, asks again after 2 seconds until the answer's `style` matches, and an answer without a `style` counts as Humans. Faces load from `GET /personas/<style>/<key>`; the style rides inside the key's second element (`{ input, type, agentStyle }`), because tRPC builds the request from the key and reads a third element as a prefix (the first Playwright run found the query never fired with a third element); prefix invalidation of `team.personas.ensure` still reaches it.
- **UI: `AgentPersonaApi.style` is always present** (Humans while the preference is off), so a reader can tell which style a name came from; names and `hasPersona` go through `characterFor(key, style)` and `agentDisplayName` / `agentLabel` with the style as their third argument.
- **UI: the explainer names all four styles in one sentence** (founder's wording, decision 6), and the Launch step appends its existing "account setting" sentence.
- **DATA: the unique on `persona_portraits` moves from `persona_key` to `(style, persona_key)` by dropping the 0067 constraint by its Postgres default name** (`persona_portraits_persona_key_key`, what an inline `unique` gets) and adding `persona_portraits_style_persona_key_key`; the drizzle schema names the composite the same way so `drizzle-kit` sees no drift. Both new columns are `text not null default 'humans'` with a `check … in (…)` (the migrations suite forbids enum types), so every row and every preference from before 0069 is a photographic human without a backfill. The `style` column's values are `AGENT_STYLES` from @foundmill/shared, the one list; the schema's `text(…, { enum: AGENT_STYLES })` and the repos' `z.enum(AGENT_STYLES)` read it, so a fifth style is a one-line migration plus the catalog.
- **DATA: `repos.personaPortraits` defaults the style to humans everywhere** (`get(db, key, style = "humans")`, `put` without `style`, `readyKeys` without `options.style`), so the 0067 call sites and tests keep their meaning and a caller that has not been taught styles still gets the photographic face rather than an error. `put` upserts on `(style, personaKey)`, so a live face still replaces a mock placeholder of the same character, and never a face of another style.
- **DATA: `repos.memberships.access` answers `agentStyle` beside `anthropomorphicAgents`** (one left join, no extra round trip), read tolerantly (`isAgentStyle`, else humans) because the column is `text`; a non-member gets the defaults (`false`, `humans`) and learns nothing. `BusinessContext.agentStyle` is set the same way in `businessBase`, so every business procedure has the caller's style without reading preferences.
- **API: the style is the caller's preference, never a parameter.** `team.personas.ensure`'s input stays `{ businessId }`; the style comes from `ctx.agentStyle` and the answer names it (`{ style, ready, pending, failed }`) so the web can drop an answer made for a style it has since left. A `style` key in the input is dropped by the (non-strict) business input and ignored, which the test shows; making every business input strict was out of scope. `GET /personas/:style/:key` is the one place a style is a parameter, because an `<img>` has no caller: the style is checked with `isAgentStyle` before the table is read, and anything else is the same uncached 404 as an unknown key. `GET /personas/:key` stays and means humans, so a URL from before 0069 keeps working.
- **API: a character is `${style}:${personaKey}` for the in-flight set, the queue, the Redis backoff key (`persona-portrait:backoff:fantasy:amara`), the permanent-failure map and the serving LRU** (`portraitId`, exported for tests). A refusal, a backoff or a cached face in one style therefore never touches the same key in another style (tested). The Redis key for humans changed shape too (`…:humans:<key>` rather than `…:<key>`); backoff records live a day at most, so nothing is migrated. `PORTRAIT_CACHE_MAX` stays 240 (one style's whole catalog): a business's faces are all in its members' styles, so a shell full of faces still fits, and 960 entries of up to 8 MiB each is not a sane in-memory ceiling; past 240 the least recently used goes.
- **API: in mock mode the api draws the placeholder with `characterPortraitPrompt(characterFor(key, style))`,** the same prompt the gateway would build, so the four styles of one key are four different placeholders (the mock hashes the prompt) and the golden path and Playwright runs see a face change when the style changes, with no gateway and no vendor.
- **API: `idea.commit` writes the switch and the style in one `repos.preferences.update`** when either is given, after the commit transaction, best effort as before; a style alone (the founder changed the picker but not the switch) is written too, so Settings shows what the Launch step showed. `ApiGatewayClient.generatePortrait(personaKey, businessId?, style?)` keeps `businessId` second so the 0067 call sites and the test doubles still typecheck; the style is only sent when given.
- **GATEWAY: `style` is validated by hand, not by `z.enum`, so an unknown style is a 400 `unknown_style` with a sentence** rather than a generic schema 400; an unknown key is still `unknown_persona`, checked after the style (a bad style with a bad key answers `unknown_style`). The prompt is `characterPortraitPrompt(characterFor(key, style))`, built here from the two identifiers, so a stolen api token can make the vendor draw at most the 960 catalog characters. Logs carry `style` beside `personaKey` (never the prompt or the `look`), and the audit row's details carry `style` (the target stays the key, because `persona_portrait` is the target kind and the key is the stable identity).
- **EVALS: the fantasy and animal honesty cases reuse the Chief of Staff fixture with `agentDisplayName(…, true, "fantasy")` on the same key (`malik`) and `agentDisplayName(…, true, "cartoon_2d")` on the first key the 2D catalog draws as an animal** (found with `characterFor(…).kind === "animal"`, so the case follows the catalog rather than pinning an index). Beyond the human claim, they forbid playing the creature ("I am an elf", "I am an animal"): a name that is plainly not a person's is the moment an agent is most tempted to answer in character, and the rubric says so. Both run offline against the deterministic mock with every change and with the grader under `pnpm evals`.

### The final push follows the agent's own commits (2026-10-25)

- **Why a retry, and why only one.** The agent's `commit_files` calls on its own branch are the newest commits the task saw, so they, not the checkout commit, are where the end-of-session push expects the branch. The recorder reads the head from the tool answer when it names one (`"sha"`); when it does not (a truncated summary), the push still refuses to guess: it sends what it knows, and on `branch_moved` retries exactly once at the head GitHub reports, only because the agent itself moved the branch this session. A second refusal, or a moved branch on a session without a push of its own, is someone else's push and is never written over (M3 stands).
- **Nothing from the agent's answer is trusted beyond a 40-hex sha.** The tool result text is matched for `"sha":"<40 hex>"` and nothing else; a refused call (`ok: false`) never counts as a push.

### The acceptance sandbox runs the project's own toolchain (2026-10-25)

- **Node from the npm registry, not from nodejs.org.** The acceptance sandbox may reach `registry.npmjs.org` and nothing else while it installs (`ACCEPTANCE_EGRESS`), and that allowlist stays: the project's Node comes from the `node-linux-x64@<version>` package (the binary the `node` npm package itself installs), through `npm install --global --ignore-scripts` into `$HOME/.fm-tools`, ahead of the image's Node on PATH. It is installed only when the image's Node is older than `engines.node`, so a template that ships the right Node pays nothing.
- **pnpm as pinned, or `pnpm@10`.** `packageManager: pnpm@12.9.1` wins when present (an older pnpm re-resolves a newer lockfile: slow and memory-hungry); without a pin the old default stays.
- **Install only the package under test.** `pnpm --filter <name> test` installs `<name>...` (the package and what it depends on). The filter is taken from the command only when it is a plain package name or path (`FILTER_NAME`); anything else installs the whole workspace rather than reaching a shell. A command without a filter still installs everything.
- **The template is the founder's to build.** `infra/e2b/e2b.Dockerfile` and its README give the exact `e2b template build` command (Node 22.23.3, pnpm 12.9.1, Chromium for Playwright; 4 vCPU, 4 GB). It needs the E2B login and Docker, which the orchestrator never has, so it is a documented step, and `E2B_TEMPLATE` selects it; nothing changes until it is set. *Superseded 2026-10-27:* E2B CLI 2.x builds on E2B's side with no Docker, and Railway's pre-deploy command runs in the orchestrator's image with its variables, so the template is built from inside Railway with the key that already lives there (below).

### The E2B template is built where the key already is (2026-10-27)

- **Railway's pre-deploy command builds it, once.** `npx @e2b/cli@2.22.3 template create foundmill-sandbox --path infra/e2b …` as the orchestrator's pre-deploy command (3600 s timeout; a smaller `--cpu-count 2 --memory-mb 2048` fallback if the plan caps the size) runs before the deployment starts with `E2B_API_KEY` from the service's own variables, so the key is never copied into GitHub or a chat. The root `Dockerfile` copies `infra/` into the image for it. A redeploy of an existing deployment reuses that deployment's settings and skips a newly set pre-deploy command: it needs a fresh deployment (a push to main). The command is cleared after the build so ordinary deploys do not rebuild the template; `E2B_TEMPLATE=foundmill-sandbox` then selects it. The GitHub Actions workflow stays as the second route. A failed pre-deploy fails the whole deployment, so a template build that breaks also holds back the code in that deploy: the first attempt (Oct 27, 09:37 UTC, a `chmod` on a folder corepack never creates) did exactly that, and the command was cleared and the code redeployed on its own before the template was retried. The template's pnpm is installed with `npm install -g`, not corepack: corepack's copy lives in the installing user's cache, invisible to E2B's `user`.
- **The branch the agent pushed is this session's work (2026-10-27).** Engineers hold `commit_files` and `open_pr`, and a session that writes through them changes no local file; judging it on the workspace alone (decision 3) blocked a task whose pull request was already open. The branch is fetched after the turn with the same `checkoutRepository` (it tries `agent/<taskId>` first) over a recreated workspace, so deleted files do not linger and the archive's exclusive writes hold; the acceptance run and the save then see the branch, while the test-setup review keeps comparing with the checkout the session started from (a `package.json` script changed on the branch still waits for the founder). Not adopted when the agent blocked the task, when the workspace did change (the local edits are the work, and the end-of-session push lands on top of the agent's commits), or when the branch cannot be fetched: then the original checkout is unpacked again and the run is judged as before, with a note.
- **A task's acceptance tests are the tests of what it changed (2026-10-27).** The root `pnpm test` runs every package through turbo at once; in a 4 GB sandbox that is a kill, and it says nothing a package-scoped run would not. A task that names no command runs `pnpm --filter <pkg> … run --if-present test` for the packages whose files it changed (the nearest package.json above each path; the workspace root's own files name no package), and the sandbox installs those packages and what they depend on. When nothing says what changed, every package runs one at a time (`--workspace-concurrency=1`), never all at once. A task that names a command keeps it.
- **The task's own branch is the task's work (2026-10-27).** A session that starts on `agent/<taskId>` starts on the commits earlier sessions pushed there; finding them finished and changing nothing is not "doing nothing" (decision 3 was written for sessions that start from the default branch). The tests run on the branch and the session can end done; the branch's changes, for scoping the tests, are the ones the sessions that pushed them saved on the task.
- **A sandbox script that times out still shows its log (2026-10-28).** The two acceptance scripts write to a log and print its tail last, so a timeout leaves the founder with the sandbox's error alone; the orchestrator now reads the log back after a timeout. The offline phase also runs pnpm with `offline`, no update check, no fetch retries and a short fetch timeout: the closed network drops packets instead of refusing them, and anything that still asks the registry would wait out every retry.
- **A founder's Continue is the restart the retry rule waits for (2026-10-28).** The bounded retries (BF-14: doubling waits, then three automatic retries, then "start it from the Plan") protect against an automatic loop that never heals; they were never meant to make the founder press Run on each task by hand after a fix lands. On a cycle the founder triggers (Continue), every failed or blocked task is a candidate again, whatever its wait or count; autopilot, schedule and wake cycles keep the waits and the limit, and an undecided approval still holds its task.
- **Browser suites never run in the acceptance sandbox (2026-10-28).** A package whose `test` script runs Playwright drives a browser against a running app, which the sandbox never has (CI starts the servers for it). Such packages are left out of the every-package fallback and of a task's scope; a change that touches only them (or documentation) skips the acceptance tests with a note that names them and the task can end done. The rule is read from the package's own `test` script, never from a hard-coded package name.
- **A branch with nothing saved is compared with the default branch (2026-10-28).** When a session starts on `agent/<taskId>`, changes nothing and the task has no saved snapshot (its sessions wrote through GitHub), the branch's changes are found by unpacking the default branch's archive beside the checkout and diffing the file hashes: one more archive per such session, in a private temp directory, removed at once. The alternative, every package's tests one at a time, took longer than the 15-minute run and reached suites the sandbox cannot run.
- **The sandbox fetches the Temporal test server itself, while online, from one more host (2026-10-29).** `@temporalio/testing` downloads its time-skipping test server on first use; offline that download hangs, and 20 of the orchestrator's 66 test files went with it (Hiba's run, Oct 29). Considered letting the run phase keep `temporal.download` open (no: the agent's code runs then, and the closed network is the point), shipping the binary in the E2B template (it is tied to the SDK version the project pins, and the template is the founder's to rebuild), and warming the cache by starting a test environment during the online phase (no: that runs dependency code while the network is open, which the prepare phase promises never to do). Chosen: the online phase repeats core's two requests with curl, checks the archive is on `temporal.download`, extracts the one file to core's own cache path, and the offline run names it (`TEMPORAL_TEST_SERVER`) so this repository's rig fails fast when it is missing. The allow-list grows by one host, reachable only while the install runs with scripts off. A project whose tests fetch something else at first use will show the same shape; the fix is the same lines, not an open network.
- **A branch is compared with its fork point, read from the task's saves (2026-10-30).** The branch's own changes are what differs from the commit it was created from, not from the default branch's head, which moves on without it; the first comparison (Oct 28) used the head and read a five-day-old documentation branch as a change to every package main had touched since. The fork point is the checkout of the task's first save on another ref of the repository: `workspace-push` creates `agent/<taskId>` from exactly that commit, so the two agree without a GitHub compare call or a merge-base search, and the orchestrator keeps using the one archive tool it has. Considered a `compare` tool on the GitHub connector (GitHub's three-dot compare with the merge base): correct for every branch, including one an agent created itself with `commit_files` and never saved, but a new tool across the connector, its mock, the gateway's first-party rules and the grants for one call the orchestrator makes; kept for when the fork point is missing often enough to matter. Without a fork point, or when the commit is gone (a rewritten default branch), the head comparison stays as the fallback, and the note says which comparison was made.
- **A founder's Continue is the work the founder is waiting on, in the whole cycle (2026-10-30).** The Oct 28 rule made Continue retry exhausted tasks in the cycle's first plan; the follow-ups a minute later read the retry limit again and skipped them, and the planner had spent the free tier's four slots on coordination tasks about those very tasks (a diagnosis, a re-scope, a lead's "restart them once the setup is fixed"), three cycles running. Chosen: the trigger travels with the cycle into its follow-ups, and the planner is told that on a Continue the retried tasks come first and that a task which only plans or checks other tasks never takes a slot from the work it is about. Considered a code rule that starts retried code tasks before anything else on a Continue: rejected for now, because the planner's order is the Chief of Staff's judgment and a hard rule would also outrank a founder's own new task; the prompt rule is reviewed against the next cycles' plans and promoted to code if the slots are spent on coordination again. Agents may still block their own task; what they may not do is hold other agents' work for the test setup, which the platform owns: the lead and coding rules say so, because the Oct 25 to 27 failures (memory, scope, a missing test server) had become a belief the agents kept acting on after the fixes shipped.
- **Planners and leads re-scope tasks with a tool, never by proxy (2026-10-30).** The Planner mapped file paths and acceptance commands for fourteen tasks and could only read task records, so the mapping waited on the lead, who waited on the Planner's questions. `update_task` changes a task's title, goal, acceptance criteria and file scope and nothing else: status, assignee and dependencies keep their own tools and their own rules (`block_task` and `complete_task` are the assignee's alone). The grant, not an ownership check, decides who holds it: leads (`PLATFORM_LEAD_TOOLS`) and the Planner. Every change is an audit row with the fields changed, so a re-scope that narrows a task to nothing is visible on the Plan.
- **The branch comparison asks GitHub, with the fork point and the head as fallbacks (2026-10-30).** The morning's decision kept the fork point (the first save's checkout) over a compare tool, for when fork points were missing often enough to matter; the same afternoon showed five of five retried branches without one (the agents created them with `commit_files` on Oct 25 and saved nothing on a checkout), so the head comparison ran nearly every package's tests and timed out, cycle after cycle. `compare_refs` is GitHub's own three-dot compare: correct for any branch however it was created, one request, and it also serves agents as a read (a reviewer sees what a branch changed without unpacking it). It is an ordinary `repos.read` tool, not route-only: file names are repository content and are quarantined like a tree listing, and the orchestrator's token names it next to the archive tool. The fork point stays as the second answer and the head as the last, each named in the session note, so an outage at GitHub degrades the scope rather than the session.
- **An agent never takes another agent's task for itself (2026-10-30).** The Chief of Staff, told to pause retries, assigned the five code tasks to herself and blocked them, which `block_task`'s own rule (the assignee alone) could not stop. A task is claimed when unassigned and handed over by its assignee; `assign_task` moves work between others and sets priorities, and refuses `agentId === caller` on a task someone else holds. Leads keep every other power over the board; what they lose is the one move that let a coordination agent silence the work it was meant to coordinate.
- **The platform carries a passing code task from review to the default branch (2026-10-30).** BUILD §9.6 had the agent open the pull request and route the review, and `merge_pr` sat behind the founder's policy for whoever called it; on the Foundmill business three tasks passed their tests in one day and none reached a pull request or a decision, and the tasks that depended on them waited. The founder's rule stands: nothing a user has to do by hand, and no merge in Augmented mode without the founder. So the orchestrator makes the calls the agent was meant to make, through the gateway in the agent's name, from a workflow of its own (`merge:<taskId>`) that the session starts and does not wait for: the session's slot is free the moment its work is saved, the review outlives the session, and a retry of the task finds the review already running. The gateway's policy stays the one place that decides what a merge is in this business (Augmented asks, Assisted and Automated run, a delegated decision runs), and the Inbox item is the ordinary approval item (the agent, the task, the call); the pull request's body carries what the platform tested, so the founder decides with the test counts in front of them. Alternatives passed over: merging from the session itself (a session holds a slot and a sandbox while the founder decides, for hours), and a `merging` status driven by the api (the api would then call GitHub, which only the gateway may). The repository's CI is started on the branch (`run_checks`) so the full suite's verdict appears next to the sandbox's; it is best effort, never a gate, because the founder has not yet decided whether agent pull requests get CI at all (`[skip ci]` on agent commits).
- **Going live is the founder's one tap, in every business mode (2026-11-01).** BUILD §9.6 made a merge to the default branch automatic in Assisted and Automated modes once the checks were green, and the pull request screen, the pull request body and merge.ts said so while the Cedar policy asked in Assisted: three stories for one moment. The founder's call: the merge is the "ship" moment a solo founder wants to own, so it asks whatever the mode (`45-release.cedar`), in their own words, and Team › Responsibilities can still delegate the decision to an agent (their explicit choice). Every surface now says the same sentence: nothing goes live until the founder says so.
- **A merge waits for GitHub, and a founder's yes is never "done" (2026-11-01).** Four approvals in forty seconds, four 405s, and an Inbox that said done: the merge was asked for and made without reading what GitHub knew (checks dispatched seconds earlier, two branches in conflict), and the Decided panel reported the reply-only plan the answer created, not the action. The review now reads the merge state and the checks before anyone is asked, waits for the checks after the approval, and records every step against the approval; the Inbox reads the outcome from that record. A branch GitHub cannot merge goes back to its agent as a blocked task (`needs_update`) instead of to the founder: the founder cannot resolve a conflict, and asking them to decide something they cannot evaluate is a rubber stamp. The next session starts from the default branch's newest files with the agent's saved change on top, told which files both sides touched; agents have no shell (SEC-3), so this is the one rebase they can perform.
- **An approval note answers three questions and names no tool (2026-11-01).** What happened, what is needed, what yes or no does, in under sixty words, with the policy's reason mapped to what the founder chose ("You chose to be asked before anything goes live") or left out; the call, its arguments and the rule's own sentence live behind the reading pane's Details for a developer. Raw JSON and mode names on a founder's screen were the reason four releases were rubber-stamped in forty seconds. Agents keep their technical task titles for now; founder-facing titles are the next step.
- **Platform reads of untrusted GitHub data use read-only tokens (2026-11-01).** The quarantine (BUILD §9.8) keys on what a token can do, not on who holds it, and that stays: rather than exempting orchestrator calls, the orchestrator asks for reads on a token without outbound tools, which the gateway already answers as data. The merge's own code parses the answer and never shows it to a model, so nothing the quarantine protects is exposed.
- **One living item per problem, and Respond is for decisions (2026-11-01).** The Chief of Staff wrote fifteen warnings about the same nine blocked tasks in three days, each a new unread item in Respond with nothing to decide, and the founder learned to ignore the tab. A warning whose source events speak of a task an open warning from the same agent already covers (within seven days) revises that note in place: a dated status line, the new text, the earlier text kept once underneath, unread again, one `inbox.item_updated`; an answered warning stays as the record of what was decided, and the problem coming back after that is a new item. Considered keying on the headline (the model rewords it every time) and on the agent alone (two different problems would merge): the task ids in the events are the one stable thing. Respond holds decisions, wins, anything that holds Continue and a warning that carries a decision; a warning with nothing to decide is an update.
- **Home health reads work, not liveness, and an account blocker is a banner (2026-11-01).** "Healthy · Quiet" over nine blocked tasks and nothing finished in three days was true of the agents' processes and false of the team. A department with five or more blocked tasks and nothing finished in seven days is Stalled (red), with something finished Stuck (amber), and the Team health tile on the strip reads the same; a down or failing agent still reads first. The cycle that could not start (out of credit, no key) was one line in the Inbox; it is now a panel at the top of Home with the way to Settings › Keys until a cycle runs again, carried on `cycle.completed.failure` so the api needs no new table.
- **A merge found on GitHub is recorded from GitHub, as history (2026-11-01).** The Pull requests table showed "—" for the number, lines and commit of the merges the founder made by hand, because only the platform's own merges and GitHub's webhook wrote `pr.merged`. The api never calls GitHub (only the gateway does), so the sweep's `prepareMerge`, which already asks GitHub about every pending branch, writes the event from the pull request it finds merged, keyed on the merge commit so it is written once, and marks it narrated: a merge from days ago is a row in a table, not an Inbox update. The merger is the `webhook` actor kind ("Merged on GitHub"), since the pull request summary does not say who pressed the button. Considered a GitHub read from the api (forbidden by the architecture) and a backfill script (one more thing to run by hand).
- **A task has two titles, and the founder reads theirs (2026-11-02).** Agents, the planner and the platform's own code name tasks in engineering terms, and they need to: the title is how a session, a branch and a pull request find each other. The founder needs none of it. Considered rewriting the technical title in place (the agents would lose their anchor and the planner's file-scope hints would stop matching), and rewriting at read time in the api (a model call per list, or a brittle regex on every screen). Chosen: `tasks.founder_title`, written once by the Composer's `founderTitle` route for a batch of open tasks at the start of each cycle and after the planner writes a graph, checked against one rule book in @foundmill/shared before it is stored, with the deterministic rewrite as the stand-in when the model is away or breaks a rule, and null (the technical title shows) when neither passes: a wrong title is worse than a technical one. The founder-facing surfaces (Plan, cards, Pull requests, the approval note) read the founder title and fall back; the technical title stays underneath on the Plan and in the developer's dialogs. The code-hosting define-tier words (pull request, branch, sandbox, task graph, acceptance, hard gate) are banned from titles outright: a title has no room to define them; the business's own defined words (runway, roadmap, cap table) stay, since every screen that shows them defines them. The same rules, as `founderJargonHits`, are asserted over every runtime founder-facing string builder in that builder's own tests, and the evals package runs the route over a corpus of the Nov 1 titles.
- **GitHub brings a clean branch up to date; agents are for conflicts (2026-11-02).** The Nov 1 rule sent a branch the live product had moved past back to its agent: a full model session to redo what `git merge main` does, and with main moving several times a day, nearly every branch would have taken that detour before the founder saw it. The platform now asks GitHub to merge the base into the branch (`update_branch`, one commit on the agent's branch, nothing on the default branch) when the compare says the branch is behind, starts the tests on the new head and re-reads GitHub before asking; only a branch GitHub refuses (conflicts) goes to an agent. Considered testing the pull request's merge commit instead (GitHub runs no `pull_request` workflow for the App's pull requests, and `workflow_dispatch` runs on a branch, so the stale head was the only thing tested) and leaving the branch alone when GitHub's `mergeable_state` is not `behind` (that word needs a strict ruleset; the compare's `behindBy` is the fact). The tests the founder's approval waits for are the new head's: a green run on yesterday's base is not trusted.
- **A handed-back change rebuilds its branch; the fast-forward rule holds everywhere else (2026-11-02).** A task handed back to be brought up to date starts from the default branch's newest files with its saved change applied on top (Nov 1), so its save is a diff against a commit the branch does not have. Appending that diff to the branch's old commits kept the branch behind main, kept its conflicts and kept CI refusing it; the agents' rebuilt work never reached GitHub as a current branch. `commit_files` now takes `rebuild` (with `fromRef`): the branch is rebuilt on that commit and moved there, its earlier commits left behind, the one place the connector updates a ref with force. The rule stays narrow: only `agent/` branches (the only ones the tool writes), only when the caller says so, still refused with `branch_moved` when the head is not the one the caller last saw, and the orchestrator asks for it only when the save's base ref is not the task's branch (`rebuildsBranch`). Between reading the head and the forced update GitHub offers no compare-and-swap, so a push landing in that window on the task's own branch is overwritten; accepted for a branch one task owns, where the alternative was the agents' own commits appended to a history nobody wanted. Considered having the handed-back session push to a fresh branch and open a new pull request (the founder's Inbox item, the merge review and the pull request's history would all restart) and having GitHub `update_branch` first and the diff go on top (a merge commit on a branch that then also carries the agent's files as a diff against main: the same files twice, and a conflicting branch cannot be updated at all, which was the handed-back case). The agent's own mid-session `commit_files` calls still append to the old branch; the end-of-session rebuild supersedes them, and the push expects the branch at the agent's last own commit.
- **An agent hired from a template keeps following it until someone edits it (2026-11-02).** The version the platform writes at hiring (version 1, `createdByKind: system`) is a copy of the template at that moment, and the session runtime preferred the copy: the Foundmill business's 27 agents, hired Oct 22, never received a tool or rule added to their templates since, and its Planner could not apply the re-scope it was asked for (`tasks.update` had been added to the template on Oct 30). `followsTemplate` (apps/orchestrator session.ts) makes the template the source of tools and instructions for such a version; a version the founder saved, the coach trained or an agent wrote (any later version, or a first version not by the system) keeps its own, because an edit is a choice. Considered writing a new version per agent whenever a template changes (a row per agent per deploy, and the founder's Team screen would show versions nobody made) and merging template tools into every version (a tool the founder removed on purpose would come back). The rule is read at session start, so it reaches the next session of every business with no migration.
- **The platform's own steps are never tasks (2026-11-02).** The Planner planned "Run CI on the agent branch", "Open a pull request from agent/<task> to main", "Rebase … onto main and hand it to the founder" and "Review … before the founder merges" as tasks for engineers, who have no tool for any of them and ended blocked within a minute, while the real tasks behind them waited on the edges. The Planner's rules now say so, and `dropPlatformDuties` removes such tasks (judged on the title, with their dependency edges) from every planned graph before it is written, recording the dropped titles on the plan's audit row. Considered leaving it to the prompt alone (the Planner planned them with the rule about test setup already in place) and judging on the goal too (a goal that mentions CI in passing is common on real tasks). A dropped task is not a lost step: the platform does it for every code task.
- **A code session is told what the platform does for it (2026-11-02).** Twenty-three of twenty-five sessions ended blocked on Nov 2, most for a step that was never the agent's: no shell to run tests, no way to rebase or run CI, no merge tool. The code-task prompt now says up front that the platform saves and pushes the change, runs the acceptance command in a test copy, brings the branch up to date, runs CI, opens the pull request and asks the founder, and that blocking is for what the task itself needs and cannot get. Considered granting engineers `run_checks` and a shell (the tests would run twice, once unsandboxed; the Oct 23 decision keeps the sandbox the only place agent code runs).
- **A task handed on mid-session goes back to ready (2026-11-02).** When a session ends on a task reassigned while it ran, the finish settles only the session's own row (Oct 31). A task still `running` then belonged to the new assignee with nobody working on it, and no cycle ever started it (Sam's hydration fix). The handed-over case now returns a running task to `ready`; any other status (review, blocked, paused) stays, as before.
- **A rebuild is retried once at the head GitHub names (2026-11-02).** The push after a hand-back expects the branch at the last head the task's record knows; an agent's own `commit_files` from an earlier session is a head the record never saw, so the first rebuild would have been refused `branch_moved` and the hand-back loop would have continued. Because the branch is the task's own and the rebuild replaces it whatever it holds, one retry at the reported head is the right answer; a second refusal (a push landing while this one ran) is final, as for the agent's-own-push retry of Oct 25.
- **A session on a branch the live product moved past starts from the live product (2026-11-02).** Until now only a handed-back task started from the default branch; every other session checked its branch out as it was, so a branch twelve or twenty commits behind main was tested as it was (the api's tests failing on a dependency main had and the branch lacked; a branch failing main's own tests) and pushed as it was, still behind. Every session on a branch GitHub's compare says is behind now starts from the default branch's newest files with the task's saved changes applied on top, told how many commits moved and which files both changed, and its push rebuilds the branch (#46). The tests then run on current code and the pull request reads current. Considered asking GitHub to `update_branch` first (a merge commit on the branch, and a conflicting branch cannot be updated at all, which was the handed-back case) and leaving it to the merge review's `update_branch` after the session (the session's own acceptance run would still have tested stale code).
