- **More checks than the list in §4, all on the turn text:** a self-introduction by the speaker's name is dropped; a claim to be human or that something was done is replaced with one fixed sentence; an unlabeled figure that is not in the brief, the remits or the founder's words is labeled "(Estimate)" / "(Forecast)" (`labelFigures`, as the foundations do). A spoken "(Estimate)" is read aloud, which is honest.
- **`teamCallDoneClaim` widens the desk's check for a call.** `doneClaimIn` reads "I've sent" and "it has been paid"; agents on a call say "we", so the call adds "we've sent", "we just booked", "the team has sent" (never inside "once", "when", "if" …, never "could we set up"). The desk's `doneClaimIn` is unchanged.
- **Hand-back is forced in three places:** an opening, a one-to-one call (nobody else can carry on, and "Let the team discuss" is team-only) and a last turn that asks the founder a question. The mock always hands back on a one-to-one call too.
- **The fallback line** is `TEAM_CALL_FALLBACK_LINE` ("Over to you: what would you like to look at first?"), from the called agent on a one-to-one call, else the Chief of Staff, else the first on the call; `fallback: true` tells the api.
- **The wrap-up never files a clarifying question or an approved spend.** `reply_only` is dropped; `approve_spend` becomes `request_human_action` (a call never approves spending; the founder approves where the spend is made), and neither is in the `action_types` the model is given. Any action whose title or params name a hard gate (`detectHardGates`, scanning what would be executed: the title and the params, a task's goal being its detail when it has none) becomes `request_human_action` with the gate's instruction and no agent.
- **Per-type param schemas live in `planParams.ts`** (`PLAN_ACTION_PARAM_SCHEMAS`, `parsePlanActionParams`), shaped to what the api executor reads: `create_task` needs a task kind and a title, a budget a whole positive amount and a channel, a department or the business, a channel action a channel key, and so on; `update_roadmap` and `set_agent_model` reuse the existing schemas and `create_playbook` uses `CreatePlaybookParams`. A task with no valid kind gets one from its words (`inferTaskKind`) rather than being dropped; anything else that does not parse is dropped. `update_roadmap` must name an item that exists (the id, else its words through `resolveRoadmapItem`) or set `create` with a title.
- **Titles:** made plain, sentence case, no closing punctuation; a title with engineering words or identifiers (`founderTitleProblems`) is rewritten with `founderPhrase`. Length is left at the schema's 200 characters (the prompt asks for ten words): cutting at ten words broke titles mid-phrase.
- **Remits:** `teamCallRemit(description)` gives the first sentence that is not an introduction ("I'm your Chief of Staff." would invite agents to introduce themselves), at most 200 characters. The api should use it for `remit`.
- **The trusted/untrusted split of the transcript:** both requests number the call (`n`) and send the founder's lines in the trusted JSON (`founder_lines`, plus `founder_just_said`) and the agents' turns inside `<untrusted_content kind="call_transcript">` with the same numbers, so the model reads the order without the founder's words ever sitting in the untrusted block. The newest `historyTurns` (40) go to the director; the wrap-up reads the whole call.
- **The mock wrap-up reads sentences, not whole founder turns,** so one "pay the invoice" never makes the rest of the turn the founder's step; it skips sentences that decline or only frame the call ("let's not …", "I want to plan the next few weeks"); a sentence that puts something on the roadmap is a new roadmap item and only that; work a sentence gives to a department ("marketing should draft …") goes to that department's agent with the kind its module does (development code, distribution channel, product research, …), read from the `departments` the request sends.
- **The subtext eval allows an agent's own job.** "That's my job" is an ordinary use (CLAUDE.md keeps "an agent's job"); any other job, employment, career, salary, cofounder or partner wording, or "you can't do it alone", fails.

## Team call: what a call files by itself, and roadmap items without the orchestrator (2026-11-12)

- **A call files writing-down work only.** When a call ends, `create_task`, `update_roadmap`, `update_spec` and `request_human_action` (the founder's own step) run at once when policy allows them (`FILED_AT_ONCE`, apps/api `services/team-call.ts`). Every other plan action the wrap-up writes (a channel switched on or off, a budget, a spend, a message or meeting with someone outside, a hire, training, a model change, a playbook) is staged in a second `team_call` plan that waits for the founder's go-ahead, even where policy alone would allow it: a call is a conversation, and switching things on is a decision the founder makes on the note, not something said in passing. Denied actions and actions in a section the person cannot write are recorded `not_filed` with the reason, never an error that loses the call.
- **Where the go-ahead lives.** The staged plan is linked to the Chief of Staff's "Notes from your team call" update (never holds Continue), so the Inbox's existing confirmation (`repos.plans.forInboxItem` → `pendingConfirmation` → `plan.confirm`) offers it; no new confirmation surface.
- **A new roadmap item is written by the api.** `executeLocally` (apps/api `services/plans.ts`) now runs `update_roadmap` with `create: true` and a title in-process, as the orchestrator does, so an item agreed on a call (or in any decision) exists at once even without Temporal; a change to an existing item stays the orchestrator's (it resolves the item by its words).
- **The call's spend ceiling** is counted as model rounds: each round's agent turns are written at one instant (`appendTurns(..., { at })`), so the rounds are the distinct instants of agent turns (`modelRoundsOf`), at most `TEAM_CALL_LIMITS.maxModelRoundsPerCall` (60); past it `say` refuses with a sentence to end the call and `continue` hands back. Two new allowances in `model-limits.ts`: `teamCall` (opening, rounds, wrap-up) and `teamCallSpeech` (each spoken turn, each transcription, each "Hear it").
- **Speech cost is recorded, not charged** for now: the gateway books spoken characters and transcription as `tool` usage rows labeled Estimate, which `bookUsage` never charges to credits; the per-business daily ceiling of 60,000 spoken characters bounds it. Package 7.1 (prices from measured cost) decides whether calls draw on credits.
- **Who chooses an accent:** owners and admins (`agent.speechAccent.set`, audited `agent.speech_accent_set`; the database trigger holds the same rule). Members and viewers hear it.

## Team call: security review fixes (2026-11-12)

The security review of the Team call backend found no hard-gate bypass; these fixes came from it.

- **Only your own words file work at once (M1).** When anyone other than the person ending the call spoke on it, everything the call agreed is staged for a go-ahead instead of filed (`onlyYourWords`); a collaborator's words never become the owner's agreement under the owner's permissions.
- **A task that reaches outside or spends waits (M2).** A `create_task` that is channel work, names a channel or carries a budget is staged, never filed `ready`, so a call cannot route around the rule that outside contact waits for the founder. The wrap-up's hard-gate scan reads every nested string of an action's params (`nestedStrings`).
- **Speech is bounded and paid for (M3).** The gateway counts transcribed audio per business per UTC day (`st:<businessId>:day`, `TEAM_CALL_LIMITS.transcriptionBytesPerBusinessPerDay`, 50 MB, the same give-back rules as spoken characters), and the api asks the platform credits gate (`assertPlatformCredits`) before every live speech, transcription and preview, whatever pays the business's models: ElevenLabs is always the platform's account. A recording is at most 1.4 MB (`maxAudioBytes`), so its base64 fits the api's 2 MB request body.
- **Call plans are retried (M4).** `repos.plans.awaitingDispatch` includes origin `team_call`, so a plan whose orchestrator part was not dispatched (Temporal down) starts on the next decision or Continue.
- **Smaller fixes.** Model rounds are counted as runs of agent turns written within a second (`modelRoundsOf`), not turns; a wrap-up left `wrapping_up` for 10 minutes (its request died) can be claimed again (`WRAP_UP_STALE_MS`); the call view hides an action's words from a reader walled from its section; `team_call.started` hides its topic from walled readers; "Hear it" audits with a nil call id. Database guards (0082): an app role starts a call only as itself, live, with fresh counters and its own business's agents (`fm_team_calls_insert_guard`), and speaks only as itself (`fm_team_call_turns_own_words`).
- **Follow-ups, not blocking:** claim a round under the row lock before the model call, so parallel requests at the limit do not each pay for one (L3); move model-written roadmap titles from the prompts' trusted JSON into the untrusted block (L6); run the "Notes from your team call" note through the Inbox redaction with Team as a wall (L1). Product question for the founder: one-to-one call transcripts are readable by everyone in the project with Team read, like the agent desk's threads.

## Team call: one-to-one calls are private by default (founder, 2026-11-12)

- **The rule.** A one-to-one call (Call on an agent's profile) is private to the person who made it: only they read the call, its transcript, its summary and its action items. Settings › Preferences › Calls has one switch, "Share my one-to-one calls" (off by default, per person, account-wide), which opens that person's one-to-one calls, past and future, to everyone on the project; switching it off makes them private again. Team calls are always the project's. The work a call files (tasks, roadmap items, plans waiting for a go-ahead) is the project's either way: privacy covers what was said, not the work.
- **Where it holds.** `team_calls.visibility` (`project | private`, 0082; a check keeps team calls `project`) is set by the api when a call starts from `account_preferences.share_one_to_one_calls`, and changed only on the service connection (`repos.teamCalls.setOneToOneVisibilityForPerson`) when the person changes the switch. RLS (`fm_team_call_visible`): a call, its turns and its action items are readable, and writable, by members only when the call is a team call, shared, or the reader's own; the generated `for all` write policy (which would also grant reads) is split into insert and update policies. A private call's `team_call.started` event carries no topic, and its Inbox note ("Notes from your call", the Inbox being the project's) lists what it filed and what waits, never the summary.
- **Migration number.** Team call merged before the branch holding 0082 and 0083, so its migration is `0082_team_calls.sql` (journal idx 70); that branch takes the numbers after it, with journal `when` values later than 1796127960000, because the migrator skips any migration older than the last one applied.

## Team call: privacy hardening from the second review (2026-11-12)

- **Sharing grants reading, never speaking.** On a one-to-one call only the person who made it speaks, ends it or changes it, even once shared (`fm_team_call_may_speak` in the call's update policy and the turn and action insert policies); on a team call every writer may.
- **Agent turns are the service's.** App roles insert only their own founder turns (`speaker = 'founder'` in the insert policy, plus `fm_team_call_turns_own_words`); the api writes the agents' turns on the service connection after the person's read of the call passed RLS. Nobody can plant an agent's line that a wrap-up would then file.
- **The database decides privacy, and fails closed.** The insert guard sets a new call's visibility from the person's own `share_one_to_one_calls` (no row reads as not shared, so private), whatever the caller sent; a team call is always `project`. `fm_set_share_one_to_one_calls(share)` (security definer, keyed on `auth.uid()`) writes the setting and that person's one-to-one calls in one statement, only in businesses they still belong to and only calls whose visibility changes. The wrap-up reads the call's visibility again in its final transaction, and a private call's Inbox note lists only filed and waiting work (no refused items, no summary).
- **What sharing off does not take back:** a note already in the project's Inbox, an event already written, a file already exported. The setting's hint says so.


## 2026-11-11 — Retention plan: measurement and the return loop

Founder decisions (2026-11-11): build the retention plan; start with measurement and the return loop; the landing-page idea box may spend platform credits (capped, rate-limited, one card each); sign-in by Google and by email with a typed code; the founder profile moves after the first idea (changes BUILD §11.1's order); the overnight shift on by default with a small cap; one-click answers from email for recommended, reversible, in-policy options, never hard gates; a daily streak.

Judgment calls in part 1:
- **Founder signals live in two places.** Business events (`founder.session_started`, `founder.notification_sent`) go through the outbox like `guidance.*`; what is about one person across days (presence, visit days) is in account-and-business tables, because the events table is per business and a report should not scan it for every ping. Pre-business signals (the landing idea box) wait for that part.
- **No event consumers: a sweep.** The services have no Redis consumers; the notifier is a Temporal schedule every 5 minutes over the database, idempotent through a unique dedupe key per founder. A crashed sweep resumes from its heartbeat.
- **Emails are templates, not model calls.** Every line is a fact from the database, cheap and never invented; spend says Estimate.
- **Who gets email.** Owners and admins (decision makers), never an example membership, never a demo business. A member's walls hold: no Inbox read, no email; walled from Customers or Money, the email names no customer, amount or milestone label.
- **The one-click rule, made strict by the security review.** Recommended, `reversible: true`, `cost_minor` 0, one recommended option, not an approval or sign-off, and the compiled plan's every action in `ONE_CLICK_ACTION_TYPES` (create a task, update the specification or roadmap, create a playbook, turn a channel off, reply), allowed by policy without asking and no hard gate. The api compiles and checks it again when it is used. The token is 256 random bits, stored as sha-256, spent before the decision, 48 hours. Viewing the page changes nothing (mail scanners follow links).
- **Pause tokens.** HMAC over `{user, expiry}` keyed by SESSION_TOKEN_SECRET under its own label (60 days); a dev secret only in development and test. A deployment that forgets both `APP_ENV` and the secret would sign pause links with the dev secret: the worst case is pausing someone's email, never access.
- **Overnight shift's cap.** Continue and Autopilot share the daily cycle and spend limits (DECISIONS 2026-11-07), so a per-night spend ceiling would also cap the founder's own Continue. The cap is therefore one cycle a night (daily cadence) with Autopilot's guard rails (credits, idle nights, hard stops, vacation). A separate per-night ceiling needs those limits split; open for the founder.
- **Desktop-only pages.** `/a/<token>` and `/email/pause` use the brand frame, which asks for a 1280 px window. The emails read on a phone; answering in one tap on a phone needs an exception to "Never build mobile layouts" for these two pages. Open for the founder.
- **Return attribution is checked.** A session counts as from a notification only when that notification went to the caller.
- **Browser notifications** follow the business stream (`customer.converted` with a payment, `goal.achieved`) while the tab is hidden; no service worker, so nothing arrives with every tab closed.
- **Beside the mobile companion (#75, merged Nov 11).** The migration is 0076 (0075 is `founder_devices`; the roadmap branch's 0076/0077 must renumber if it lands after this). Email switches live in their own map, `account_preferences.email_notifications`, beside #75's push `notifications`, read by `founder.email.*` (push stays `founder.preferences.notifications`). Email keeps its own kinds for now (the briefs and credits have no push kind); folding the shared kinds (waiting/decision, win/customer, run_finished, milestone/goal) into one vocabulary is a follow-up. The email notifier stays a scheduled sweep (waiting 30 minutes, the brief's hour and the lookback need a clock), not the push notifier's outbox hook. "Since you were last here" is its own read, `today.sinceLastVisit`, rather than a field on `today.get`, which has a round-trip budget test; the app reads it from there. #75's OTP sign-in covers the typed-code half of the sign-in decision. Email links use `https://<APP_URL>/b/<businessId>/…` paths, which the app can claim as universal links; `/a/<token>` answers the same from the web or the app.


## 2026-11-12 — Retention, part 2: the limits split, phone pages, the streak

Founder, 2026-11-12: "Split the limits, allow phone pages, then do the streak."
- **What "split" means.** The shared daily cycle cap and spend ceiling (DECISIONS 2026-11-07) still bound Continue and Autopilot alike; Autopilot gains two limits of its own that only its scheduled cycles count. A Continue run's follow-on rounds are started by the same workflow with trigger `autopilot`, so "Autopilot's own" is read from `autopilot.cycle_started` without a `runId`, not from the trigger.
- **A dollar cap that holds while the cycle runs.** The own spend ceiling is checked before a start and is also the cycle's budget (`budgetCapMinor`); each session gets its reserve as `spendCapMinor`, so what the sessions may spend never adds up past the cap. The Chief of Staff's plan for the cycle is paid before dispatch and counts toward the next check, so a night can run a few cents over (Estimate). A session stopped at its share ends `blocked` (budget) and its task is planned again in the next cycle, like any blocked task.
- **The overnight shift's numbers.** One cycle and $0.15 a night (about $4.50 a month), inside the free tier's $5 of credits; the founder can change both.
- **Phone pages.** Only `/a/<token>` and `/email/pause`, the two pages a founder email opens; each is one panel with one button, so no new layout was designed. The RFC 8058 `/email/unsubscribe` is a POST handler and has no page.
- **The streak.** Days in a row the founder opened the business (the presence ping's visit days, in the business's calendar). A vacation day freezes it (neither breaks nor adds), because the founder chose to step away while the team works. Today never breaks a streak before it ends: a streak that reached yesterday is alive this morning, "at risk". Milestones at 3, 7, 14, 30, 60, 100, 200 and 365 days get their own line; gold only on that day. It is its own read, `today.streak`, like `today.sinceLastVisit` (the mobile app reads both there).

## 2026-11-12 — One sender for every email from Foundmill (founder decisions)

Founder decisions, 2026-11-12 (confirmed in their own words, not relayed), on joining the retention work to the rebuild's email:
1. **Migrations renumbered and rebased.** The retention migrations are `0083_founder_return.sql` and `0084_autopilot_own_limits.sql` (journal idx 71 and 72, `when` 1796128020000 and 1796128080000), after #79 (0076–0081 are the rebuild's) and Team call's `0082_team_calls` (founder, 2026-11-12: "merge team call first"). Their `when` must be later than every migration before them: the migrator skips an entry older than the last one applied, without an error.
2. **One sender, one preference.** Every email Foundmill sends a person about their own project goes through the gateway's `POST /v1/notify/email` on the `platform_notify` connector. The retention work's `founder_mail` connector, `/v1/founder-mail/send`, `FOUNDER_EMAIL_FROM`, `FOUNDER_EMAIL_FROM_NAME` and `FOUNDER_MAIL_LIVE_KEY` are deleted. The switches live in one place, `account_preferences.notifications.email` (the `email_notifications` column is gone from 0082), one per kind: `decision_waiting`, `win`, `milestone`, `run_finished`, `credits`, `daily_brief`, `weekly_review`.
3. **When a waiting decision is emailed.** 30 minutes after the person leaves, only when it holds up the team's work; every other waiting note waits 24 hours.
4. **One limit.** 3 emails a day per person across every kind and project; the morning brief and the weekly review don't count toward it.
5. **Small screens.** `/a/<token>` and `/email/pause` join the small-screens rule in CLAUDE.md (and `SMALL_SCREEN_PREFIXES`) instead of a separate exception; `lib/phone-pages.ts` and `BrandFrame allowNarrow` are gone.
Also kept as they are (relayed, then confirmed): the rebuild's limit of 1 email a day per person and project, and its deployment-wide ceiling (`pn:all:day`, `PLATFORM_NOTIFY_DAILY_CEILING`).

Judgment calls:
- **"Holds up the team's work"** is `repos.notifyEmail.holdingUpWork`: the note holds Continue (`blocks_continue`), or an unresolved approval or a pending spec or roadmap sign-off waits on it. Those are the notes without which the team stops; a plain question is not.
- **"After I leave"** is measured from the later of the note's arrival and the person's last ping in that business, so a note that arrives while they are away goes out 30 minutes after it arrived, and nothing goes out while they are here (`present`).
- **Who decides, who sends.** The api's notify pass (now every 5 minutes) decides waiting decisions; the orchestrator's `founder-notifications` sweep decides the other kinds. Both send ids only. The gateway reads everything again, checks the walls and the pause, counts the limits in Redis, builds the words (`decisionWaitingEmail`, and `retentionEmail`, the old orchestrator renderer moved into packages/shared with `withoutLinks` on every piece of user or agent text) and keeps the record (`founder_notifications`, tokens removed). The orchestrator no longer sees an address.
- **The 1-a-day-per-project limit applies to the limited kinds together**, so a win and a waiting decision in one project on one day is one email; the briefs bypass both per-person limits but not the ceiling.
- **Retention emails need a started project** (`loadNotifyFacts` is null before commit: `not_started`); a waiting decision does not.
- **Skips.** A refusal that will not change (turned off, paused, nothing new, no address, not a member...) is recorded on `founder_notifications` as skipped, so the sweep stops asking; one that may (a limit, `busy`, `store`, `present`, the vendor) is asked again next sweep within the day's lookback.
- **`founder.notification_sent` is gone**; `notify.email_sent` carries `notificationId` and `offersAnswer`, and its `inboxItemId` is null for a brief or a finished run (never the notes a brief lists, so each can still get its own email).
- **Settings keeps one Email section**, one switch per kind (`email-<kind>-switch`), the brief's hour, browser notifications, the pause and the latest emails. The off page and the toast name the kind.

## Team call in production: microphone and audio headers (2026-11-12)

The first production call showed two gaps the mock-mode tests could not: the web's `Permissions-Policy` said `microphone=()`, so browsers refused the microphone without ever asking, and the Content-Security-Policy had no `media-src`, so the spoken turns (played from `data:` URLs) fell back to `default-src 'self'` and were blocked silently. The policy now says `microphone=(self)` (the app's own pages; the browser still asks the person) and `media-src 'self' data: blob:`. Speech, transcription and the accent preview go one per request (`UNBATCHED_PROCEDURES`, apps/web `lib/trpc.ts`), so the first agent's audio never waits for the second's in a shared batch. Measured on that call: ElevenLabs answered each turn in 13 to 18 seconds; streaming the audio (ElevenLabs' `/stream` endpoint) is the follow-up if that stays slow.

## Team call: streamed speech (2026-11-12)

The first production call measured 13 to 18 seconds before each turn could play: the buffered `POST /text-to-speech/{voice_id}` answers only once the whole turn is rendered, and the api then sent it as base64. Turns now stream end to end. The gateway's `POST /v1/speech/stream` (service JWT, api only) runs the buffered route's checks in the same order (accent, words, the business's window, the day's spoken characters, a durable store) and then ElevenLabs' `/text-to-speech/{voice_id}/stream` (`createElevenlabsSpeechStreamer`, packages/connectors, outside the tool contract because a tool answers one JSON result), passing the MP3 bytes on as they come; the audit row `gateway.speech.streamed` and the usage Estimate are written as the stream starts. An `<audio>` element sends no bearer, so `teamCall.speech({ stream: true })` makes every check a speech request makes (Team section, RLS on the call and the turn, the credits gate), renders the words with the listener's names and issues a random ticket (`services/speech-tickets.ts`: Redis when the api has one, two minutes, at most three requests); `GET /team-call-audio/:ticket` on the api pipes the gateway's stream (`no-store`, `same-site`, a sandboxing CSP). The room asks for a stream for every turn, plays it at once from the api's origin (the web CSP's `media-src` names it), and asks once for the whole clip if a stream cannot play. Simulated speech never streams.
- **Streamed speech hardening (security review, same day).** A ticket serves two requests (the first and one retry), each a new paid vendor call, so the credits gate runs on each request as well as at issue; the Redis use counter is created with its expiry when the ticket is issued; store errors leave the store as a fixed message without the command's arguments; a store that is down falls back to the whole clip; every logged or traced path masks the ticket (`loggedPath`, the request log, traces, `notFound` and `onError`); the gateway's stream route has the service body limit; the api ends a stream that sends nothing for 15 seconds (`withIdleTimeout`) and the player lets go of a stream when its turn ends. Accepted: a ticket is not revoked within its two minutes when someone leaves the business, and Railway's edge logs keep full paths.

## Account events: a private, person-level event store (founder, 2026-11-12)

The founder decided on Nov 12, 2026 to build a person-level event store before the network features need it. Until now every event belonged to a project (`events.business_id` and `outbox.business_id` are NOT NULL, streams are keyed by business, every member reads the rows), so a fact about a person had nowhere to go without showing that person to a project's members. The contract is docs/ARCHITECTURE.md §4 (0085) and §5 "Account events", §12 for the wall. The judgment calls:

- **One catalog, scope on the declaration.** Every declaration carries `scope`. `declareEvent` declares a business-scoped event and states `scope: "business"` itself; the 69 existing calls were not edited (one more line in each would conflict with every branch that touches the catalog; a call may still write `scope: "business"` out). An account-scoped event is declared with `declareAccountEvent`, which is the only way to get `scope: "account"`. Rejected: a second catalog or a second type list; an optional `scope` that silently defaults (the helper's name now says which).
- **`ACCOUNT_EVENT_TYPES` in packages/shared.** The same fact as the declarations' scope, as a subset tuple beside `PERSON_ADDRESSED_EVENT_TYPES` and `GUIDANCE_EVENT_TYPES`, because packages/db sits below the catalog and has to refuse these types in `repos.events.write` (the function every service's business write ends in). The catalog's `satisfies` clause and a check at load fail when the two disagree, so they cannot drift. Rejected: deriving the scope from the catalog alone (packages/db could then not refuse anything).
- **The business types narrowed in place.** `PlatformEvent`, `WriteEventInput`, `buildEvent`, `writeEvent` and `toWire` now take `BusinessEventType`. The wrappers that pass a generic type through (`emit` and `writeEventRow` in the api, `writeEventRow` in the composer, `writeGatewayEvent`, `emitEvent`, the connectors' `makeEvent`) change one token each. The consumer API (`consume`, its options and handler types) is source-compatible. Rejected: generics that accept every type and fail on a `never` field (unreadable errors, and a wrapper could still be typed to take an account type).
- **Separate tables, append-only.** `account_events` and `account_outbox`, keyed by `user_id` with cascade; `outbox` is not altered and `events` gains one check and nothing else (the review entry below; the migration test compares their catalog before and after). No `entity_ids` (CRM records are a project's) and no `narrated` (the Composer writes a project's Inbox). An event is never updated: the guard trigger refuses UPDATE for every role, and `service_role` holds only SELECT and INSERT on it. The queue row is tied to its event's own person by a composite foreign key. Rejected: a nullable `events.business_id` (every policy, index and reader of `events` would have to learn a second kind of row, and a mistake in any of them is a leak).
- **Column grants.** A person reads `id, user_id, type, occurred_at, payload, created_at` of their own rows. `actor` stays with the services (an internal name), and so does `idempotency_key` (a key is often built from ids, and for a fact about two people that can be the other person's account id). `updated_at` is bookkeeping. The queue is service role only.
- **The two-person rule, where it can be enforced.** At declaration (schema conventions checked as the catalog loads), at write (values), in the database (a trigger and a check, whichever writer) and in tests. The database part looks up every UUID in a payload in `auth.users` by primary key, so "no payload carries an account id" holds even for a writer that skips the package. A UUID that is no account's is the writer's to judge (`recordIds`). Email addresses are refused by the writer only: the database has no cheap way to tell an address from text, and a blanket pattern in SQL would be a second copy of a rule that will need tuning. First rejected, then adopted after the security review (the entry below): a check constraint that lists the account-scoped types.
- **One writer.** `writeAccountEvent` in packages/events does everything and works on the real tables; `repos.accountEvents` has no `write`, so the checks cannot be stepped around through a second path. (The business `writeEvent` in packages/events still inserts an outbox row without `business_id` and still fails on the real tables; services go through `buildEvent` and `repos.events.write`. That older mismatch is not changed here.)
- **Idempotency.** `on conflict do nothing` on the partial unique index, then a read of the existing id. The business path (`repos.events.write`) selects and then inserts, so two writers racing on one key leave the second with a unique violation to handle. The account writer never raises for a repeat, raced or not.
- **The relay: its own loop, and failures on the row.** The loop moved to `outbox/relay-loop.ts` unchanged and both relays use it. The business relay never writes `outbox.attempts` or `outbox.last_error` (the columns have been unused since 0011); the account relay does, and adds `next_attempt_at` so a failing row is retried with its own backoff and never read on every tick. A row that fails does not fail the batch: one person's stream never holds up another person's events. A row that can never be valid is closed and noted on `dlq:account-outbox-relay` without its payload (the business relay copies the whole row to its dead-letter stream; a person's event is not copied to a stream that outlives their account). Rejected: closing a row after N attempts (a Redis outage would close everything waiting).
- **`ae:{userId}`, not `u:{userId}`.** `u:` is content-free, written straight after a commit, best effort, capped at 200 entries and delivered raw by apps/stream; clients and the mobile app read its frames as signals. Account events carry payloads, are delivered at least once from an outbox, are read by consumer groups (a group on `u:*` would have to skip signals, and a burst of signals would trim events away) and need a wall that checks every entry. A key of its own leaves every line of the signal path as it was.
- **Stream retention of 7 days.** Shorter than a business stream's 30: the stream is a copy of private facts outside the database, and the rows cascade at once when an account is deleted while the copy lives until its TTL. `deleteAccountStream` removes it at once; the api has no account-deletion flow to call it from yet.
- **The stream wall checks every entry.** The authorizer opens `ae:<userId>` to that account only; `viewAccountEvent` then drops anything on the stream that is not that account's own account event and sends only the fields the person may select. `EVENT_WALLS` files the business-scoped types only, and an account-scoped type on a business stream is dropped for every reader, the way a person-addressed one is.
- **Dead letters without payloads.** `consume` gained `deadLetterRecord`; `consumeAccountEvents` keeps a reason code, the event's id and its type (the review entry below). The business consumer's dead letters are unchanged.
- **Export and deletion.** The export is per business and classifies tables with a `business_id`; the account tables have none, so they are in neither list and a business's archive cannot ask for them. There is no account-level export (the other account tables, `account_preferences`, `founder_devices` and `person_handle_releases`, are not exported either); `repos.accountEvents.list` pages a person's events for the day there is one.
- **The first two events.** `profile.published` and `profile.unpublished` are written on a change of state only (a save or a second publish while public writes nothing), in the same transaction, with the handle and nothing else. A change of handle while public writes no event (no third type was asked for), so an old `profile.published` can carry a former handle. Nothing is backfilled for profiles that were already public.
- **An address that is, or holds, an id is refused** (`handleLooksLikeAnId` in the api; widened after the review, the entry below). The address rule allows letters, digits and hyphens, and the two-person rule refuses anything shaped like an id in a payload, so such a handle could not be published without failing its event. New handles of that shape are refused with the reserved-address sentence, and a stored one cannot go public. Taking a profile down never depends on its event.
- **Migration number.** `0085_account_events.sql`, journal idx 71, `when` 1796128380000 (agreed with the other teams on Nov 12; it merges after 0083 and 0084, which are not on main yet, and idx and `when` are reconciled at the rebase before merge). The number appears in the file name, the journal, `TAG` in `packages/db/src/__tests__/migration-0085.test.ts` (and that file's name), the tag list in `migrations.test.ts`, comments in `schema/governance.ts`, `repos/account-events.ts`, `rls/tables.ts` and `rls/account-events.test.ts`, and in ARCHITECTURE, PROGRESS and this entry.
- **Found on the way.** `pnpm -w turbo typecheck` can report a stale pass: `typecheck` has `dependsOn: []`, and a cached result for a package was replayed after a package it imports had changed (apps/orchestrator and apps/composer passed from cache while `tsc` failed in both). The checks for this change were run with `--force`.

## Account events: fixes from the security review (2026-11-12)

The security review of the account events branch found no blocker, three things to fix before the pull request and four cheap hardenings. What changed, and why each was decided the way it was. The entry above was corrected where these fixes made a sentence untrue.

- **The database lists the account-scoped types, and `events` refuses them (the one sanctioned change to `events`).** The review reproduced a signed-in member inserting `profile.published` straight into `events` under RLS, where every member then read it: only TypeScript stood in the way. `fm_account_event_types()` returns the list once; `events_not_account_scoped` keeps those types out of `events` and `account_events_type_account_scoped` lets only those types into `account_events` (the database did not restrict `type` there before). This follows 0060's `events_not_person_addressed`. A row planted under one of these names before the migration is deleted first so the check can be added (no writer ever produced these types; its outbox row cascades). Nothing else about `events` changes, and nothing about `outbox`: `migration-0085.test.ts` now asserts exactly one added constraint and an otherwise identical catalog and rows. The cost, accepted: a new account-scoped type needs a migration that redefines the function, and a test holds the SQL list equal to `ACCOUNT_EVENT_TYPES`. This reverses the earlier call against listing types in SQL.
- **"Carries an account id" means any spelling of it in one value.** The trigger and the writer matched only the hyphenated form; an id without hyphens, with hyphens elsewhere or with Unicode dashes went through, and so did a system actor named by a compact id or carrying extra keys. Now the database reads the payload's text in lower case with an id's punctuation out (hyphen, ten Unicode dashes, space, dot, colon, underscore) and looks up every run of 32 or more letters a to f and digits in `auth.users` at every position, so an id inside a longer run is found as well. Spaces, dots, colons and underscores were added beyond what the review asked: JSON's quotes, commas and brackets still separate values, and a false alarm needs text that spells an existing account's id. The writer folds fullwidth characters too, finds the id of anyone the fact is about through any separator, and refuses anything shaped like an id outside a declared record id. The actor is exactly `{ kind, id }`, both text; a system actor is a lowercase word. The first version of the new check passed an actor with no `id` (a missing key made the expression null, and a check passes on null); the check is now wrapped in `is true`, and a test covers it.
- **What cannot be enforced is written down.** An id cut into pieces across several values, an id in another encoding, and private facts that are not ids are beyond both checks. ARCHITECTURE §5 says so; the schema conventions and the review of each new declaration are what stand against them.
- **A handle that holds an id.** The first rule was anchored (a handle that is a UUID), so `x` followed by a UUID passed, could be set while public (a change of address while public writes no event) and then made the take-down's event fail, leaving the profile public. Two fixes. `handleLooksLikeAnId` now refuses any address that holds 32 letters a to f and digits in a row once the hyphens are out (the account events' own `looksLikeAnId`), at save and at publish, with the reserved-address sentence; this also refuses a bare 32-character address, which could be someone's account id. And taking a profile down never depends on its event: the event is built first and written in a savepoint, and when either fails the profile is taken down and the event skipped, with a warning that carries the account id and a reason code. The rule lives in the api, not in `founder_profiles` (no check constraint was added to that table here).
- **Errors never repeat a key or a value.** Zod's text for a strict object is `Unrecognized key: "<the key>"`, and that text reached `account_outbox.last_error`, the relay's dead letter and the api's warn log; the comment that said otherwise was wrong. Reasons are now built from issue codes and depths (`issueReason`), the writer's refusals carry a rule code and a depth (`AccountRuleError`), and an unknown type is not echoed. The envelope's own field names may be said, because the schema wrote them. Declaration-time errors still name the schema's keys: a developer wrote those.
- **Consumer dead letters.** `consume`'s option became `deadLetterRecord` (it replaces the entry's fields, the stream name, the entry id and the error text together); an account dead letter keeps a reason code, the event id when it is a UUID, the type when it is account-scoped, and the group, consumer, delivery count and time. A handler's own error becomes `handler_failed`.
- **The trigger's search path** is empty and every name in it is schema-qualified; the two helper functions are the same.
- **`account_events.updated_at` stays.** Every UPDATE is refused, so the column and its trigger never do anything; they stay because the convention test `migrations.test.ts` "every table has created_at; every table but audit_log has updated_at with its trigger" requires them of every table. Exempting the table there was the alternative; one convention without a second exception was preferred.
- **Follow-ups, recorded and not built.**
  - Idempotency keys may embed another person's account id (`follow:<id>`) and stay on the row after that person's account is deleted. The column is not readable by clients; hash the keys later.
  - Nothing calls `deleteAccountStream`, because no account-deletion flow exists; and a relay write racing a deletion can recreate `ae:{userId}`, which then lasts up to 7 days.
  - A queue row that fails forever is retried every 5 minutes with no alert; `repos.accountEvents.outboxFailing` is the read an alert would use.
  - Events for one person can arrive out of order after a transient failure (a failed row is retried after newer rows were published); consumers that need order must sort by `occurredAt`.
  - A future event with client-supplied record ids could turn the trigger's distinct error into an "is this UUID an account" oracle. Writers must check record ids against their own table first, and the api must map that error to a generic one.
  - None of this was run on real Postgres or Supabase (PGlite only). The definer trigger under `service_role`, EXECUTE on `fm_account_event_types()` for `authenticated` inside the check on `events`, and the platform's default privileges need one check on a real project before the migration is applied to production.

## 2026-11-12 — The account relay is turned off: production's database pool wedged from the deploy that started it

[reidjxn/foundmill#86](https://github.com/reidjxn/foundmill/pull/86) reached production at 19:20 UTC (the api applies migrations and deploys on merge). From that deploy on:
- the api logged "N of 8 warm-up queries did not answer within 10000 ms: a pooled connection may be wedged" about once a minute, climbing from 1 to 8;
- the pool watchdog found the pool wedged while the database answered outside it and restarted the api at 19:44 and 19:49, and by its design would keep doing so about every five minutes;
- both shutdowns of the new code waited the whole 45 s on "stop outbox relay" and skipped closing Redis and the database, where every shutdown of the two days before took 0 to 19 ms.
Nothing of the kind was logged from Nov 10 to 19:19 on Nov 12. Postgres itself logged only checkpoints.

- **What is known.** It is the client-side loss of pooled connections described on 2026-10-19 (postgres.js), now at a rate of about one a minute. The only thing #86 added that runs on a timer in the api is the account relay, a second poller on the same pool every 250 ms beside the business relay.
- **What is not known.** Why a second poller leaks connections when one did not. It did not reproduce here: the same supervisor on a local Postgres 16 and Redis, idle and with rows, and the whole api from main on a local Postgres with the real schema for 100 s, all stopped in milliseconds with no stuck session.
- **Decision.** The account relay does not start (`ACCOUNT_RELAY_ENABLED = false` in `apps/api/src/services/relay.ts`). Account events are still written with their outbox rows, so nothing is lost; they are relayed when it is turned back on. No client reads `ae:{userId}` yet, so nothing a person sees changes. Migration 0085 stays: its tables, checks and trigger act only on writes.
- **Before it is turned back on:** find the cause on a deployment with production's latency and load (not a laptop), and fold the account outbox into the business relay's own tick (one loop, one query after the other) so the pool carries one poller as it did before.
- **What this session got wrong.** The pull request said the migration should be checked on a real database "before it is applied to production" and was merged without that check, and without knowing that a merge to main is a deploy. A merge is a deploy here: the check comes before the merge.

## A home for a person with no project (Nov 12, 2026; THESIS_REVIEW 2.4)

- **Its own address, `/home`, not `/`.** A signed-out `/` is the landing page, which stays desktop until 2.1/2.7, and the small-screen rule is decided by path (`SMALL_SCREEN_PREFIXES`); one address for the person's home lets it work from 390 px without opening the landing page too. The signed-in index still answers `/` and sends a person with no project there.
- **Start something leads; finding people is an offer beside it.** The card says the person can do all of it on their own (CLAUDE.md "Solo is complete"). No people are suggested anywhere on the home, for anyone: the find card is a way to go and look, not a list. Reading `lookingFor` to tailor it waits until there is something to tailor.
- **Closed people layer.** Where LIVE is off (production today), the find card says "Opening soon" without a link, and the waiting counts are not read: nobody can have written to the person.
- **Onboarding still continues to /new.** A person who has just finished the profile is mid-way through starting; the home is where they land on a later visit. Revisit with 2.3's three questions ("start something", "join something", "not sure yet").
- **The `(live)` layout is left to the network session.** Its pages call `useBusiness()` (PersonalNotice, connect, community and profile screens), so working without a project is a change to those screens, which that session owns.
